Strong uniqueness of tangent flows at cylindrical singularities in Ricci flow
Hanbing Fang, and, Yu Li

Abstract
In this paper, we establish a Lojasiewicz inequality for the pointed 𝒲 \mathcal{W} -entropy in the Ricci flow, under the assumption that the geometry near the base point is close to a standard cylinder ℝ k × S n − k \mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k} or the quotient thereof. As an application, we prove the strong uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow at the first singular time of the Ricci flow. Specifically, we show that the modified Ricci flow near the singularity converges to the cylindrical model under a fixed gauge.



1
Introduction

Ricci flow, introduced by Richard Hamilton in 1982 in his foundational paper
[
Ham82
]
, is a geometric evolution equation for Riemannian metrics. Given an initial Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g_{0})
, the Ricci flow evolves the metric according to the nonlinear PDE:

\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g(t)).

This equation may be viewed as a heat-type flow that tends to uniformize the geometry of the manifold by smoothing out curvature irregularities over time. Ricci flow has had a profound impact on geometric analysis and topology, culminating in Grigori Perelman’s resolution of the Poincaré and Geometrization Conjectures (
[
Per02
,
Per03b
,
Per03a
]
).

In many cases, the development of short-time singularities is inevitable and has become a central focus of study. A short-time singularity is formed at the first singular time, near which the curvature of the Ricci flow is unbounded. Understanding the structure of such singularities is crucial for extending the Ricci flow past singular times, whether through geometric surgeries or via weak flow techniques.

A key strategy in analyzing singularities is blow-up analysis—rescaling the flow at the singularity to extract
tangent flows
. For Type I singularities, it follows from the work of Enders-Müller-Topping
[
EMT11
]
that any blow-up sequence converges smoothly to a self-similar ancient solution, specifically a Ricci shrinker. More generally, Bamler
[
Bam20b
]
showed that blow-up sequences
\mathbb{F}
-converge to a Ricci shrinker with mild singularities, which is referred to as a metric soliton.

In
[
FL25a
]
, a spacetime distance
d^{*}
was introduced for closed Ricci flows with entropy bounded below (see Definitions
2.9
and
2.16
). Using this notion, the time-zero slice—corresponding to the singular time—can be obtained as the metric completion under
d^{*}
. Specifically, consider a smooth closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
, where
t=0
is the first singular time. Then the space

\displaystyle(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})

is defined as the metric completion of
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)}
with respect to
d^{*}
. It can be shown (see
[
FL25a
, Section 9]
) that the completion only adds new points in the time-zero slice
Z_{0}
, and that
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space in the sense of
[
FL25a
]
, with singularities entirely contained in
Z_{0}
.

In this framework, a tangent flow
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
at a point
z\in Z_{0}
is defined as a pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
for a sequence
r_{j}\searrow 0
. It is proved in
[
FL25a
, Section 7]
that
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
is itself a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space defined over
(-\infty,0]
. Moreover, its regular part
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}}_{t})
, which is given by a Ricci flow spacetime, satisfies the Ricci shrinker equation

\mathrm{Ric}(g^{Z^{\prime}})+\nabla^{2}f_{z^{\prime}}=\frac{g^{Z^{\prime}}}{2|\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}|},

where
f_{z^{\prime}}
is the potential function at
z^{\prime}
(see Definition
2.6
). For further properties of tangent flows, we refer readers to
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.9]
.

A fundamental question in the analysis of geometric PDEs is whether the tangent space at a singularity is unique. When a singularity forms, one typically performs a blow-up procedure to study the local behavior. However, depending on the choice of rescaling sequences, non-uniqueness of tangent spaces is in principle possible. Conversely, uniqueness suggests that the underlying geometric and analytic structure is stable and coherent at all smaller scales.

Significant progress has been made in establishing the uniqueness of tangent spaces across various geometric contexts. For minimal surfaces, Allard and Almgren
[
AA81
]
in 1981 proved the uniqueness of tangent cones under the assumption of smooth cross-sections and integrability conditions. This integrability assumption was later removed by Leon Simon in 1983
[
Sim83
]
. White
[
Whi83
]
further showed that tangent cones are unique for two-dimensional area-minimizing integral currents.

In the setting of Ricci-flat manifolds with maximal volume growth, Cheeger and Tian
[
CT94
]
proved the uniqueness of tangent cones at infinity under quadratic curvature decay and an integrability assumption. Colding and Minicozzi later showed in
[
CMI14
]
that if one tangent cone has a smooth cross-section, then the tangent cone at infinity is unique. They also established the uniqueness of tangent cones at singular points in Gromov–Hausdorff limits of noncollapsed Einstein manifolds, again assuming the smoothness of the cross-section of a tangent cone. In the Kähler–Einstein setting, Donaldson and Sun
[
DS17
]
proved the full uniqueness of tangent cones, without assuming smooth cross-sections.

In mean curvature flow, tangent flows correspond to self-shrinkers, and uniqueness means that a particular self-shrinker is the unique blow-up limit at a singularity. Several notable results have established the uniqueness under various geometric assumptions. Schulze
[
Sch14
]
proved uniqueness when one tangent flow is a compact smooth shrinker. Colding and Minicozzi
[
CMI15
,
CMI23
]
showed that uniqueness holds when one tangent flow is the standard cylinder
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k}
. More recently, Chodosh–Schulze
[
CS21
]
and Lee–Zhao
[
LZ24
]
established the uniqueness in the case where the tangent flow is an asymptotically conical self-shrinker.

The uniqueness of tangent flows in Ricci flow remains a subtle and evolving topic. When a tangent flow at a singularity is given by a compact, smooth Ricci shrinker, it has been shown by Sun–Wang
[
SW15
]
and Chan–Ma–Zhang
[
CMZ24
]
that the tangent flow is unique. More recently, Choi–Lai
[
CL25
]
obtained sharper convergence rates under the additional assumption that the Ricci shrinker is integrable. In the setting of Fano manifolds, the tangent flow at the single singularity is also known to be unique; see
[
CSW18
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
HL24
, Corollary 1.4]
. However, in general, most tangent flows are non-compact, and the question of uniqueness in this broader context remains significantly more delicate and technically challenging.

One effective approach to proving the uniqueness of tangent flows is to study the
rigidity
of Ricci shrinkers in the moduli space of all shrinkers. Specifically, if a Ricci shrinker is isolated in this space—meaning no other nearby Ricci shrinkers exist—then, via a continuity argument, one can deduce that if a tangent flow is given by this shrinker, then it must be the unique tangent flow. This argument is plausible because all tangent flows at a singularity are derived from a single underlying closed Ricci flow, and the blow-up limits vary continuously under suitable topologies.

The first example of a rigid non-compact, nontrivial Ricci shrinker was given by Li–Wang
[
LW24
]
, who proved the rigidity of the standard cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times S^{n-1}
. Subsequently, Colding–Minicozzi
[
CMI25
]
established the rigidity of the cylinders
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k}
for all
1\leq k\leq n-2
. Later, Li–Zhang
[
LZ23
]
extended the techniques of
[
CMI25
]
to prove the rigidity of more general cylinders of the form
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times N^{n-k}
and their quotients, where
N
is an
H
-stable Einstein manifold with obstruction of order
3
(see
[
LZ23
, Definitions 2.11, 2.20]
).

These rigidity results yield powerful consequences for tangent flow uniqueness. In particular, Colding–Minicozzi
[
CMI25
, Theorem 1.2]
proved that, under the Type I curvature assumption, the tangent flow at a singularity is unique if one tangent flow is given by
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k}
. More generally, Li–Zhang
[
LZ23
, Theorem 6.2]
showed that the tangent metric soliton at a singularity is unique if one of such blow-up limits is a generalized cylinder or its quotient. Here, the notion of a tangent metric soliton refers to a blow-up limit in the
\mathbb{F}
-convergence sense introduced by Bamler
[
Bam20b
]
.

Building on these rigidity results, one obtains the following uniqueness theorem for cylindrical tangent flows in the framework of Ricci flow limit spaces in
[
FL25a
]
. Here, the definition of isometry can be found in
[
FL25a
, Definition 5.21]
.

Theorem 1.1

(Uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow)
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be the completion of a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. For any
z\in Z_{0}
, if a tangent flow at
z
is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
, then every tangent flow at
z
is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

The proof of Theorem
1.1
will be given later in Theorem
2.23
, and is based on the aforementioned rigidity and convergence results. Here, we define the model cylindrical Ricci shrinker

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{k}_{-1}:=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times S^{n-k},g_{E}\times g_{S^{n-k}},\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{n-k}{2}+\Theta_{n-k}\right),

where
g_{E}
is the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
g_{S^{n-k}}
is the round metric on
S^{n-k}
such that
\mathrm{Ric}(g_{S^{n-k}})=g_{S^{n-k}}/2
. The vector
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
denotes the standard coordinate function on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The constant
\Theta_{n-k}
is the entropy of the cylinder.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{k}=(\bar{M},(\bar{g}(t))_{t<0},(\bar{f}(t))_{t<0})
denote the corresponding Ricci flow with potential such that
t=0
is the singular time. Then,
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
is defined as the completion of
(\bar{M},(\bar{g}(t))_{t\in(-\infty,0)})
with respect to the spacetime distance
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. The base point
p^{*}
is taken to be the limit of
(\bar{p},t)
as
t\nearrow 0
, where
\bar{p}
is a minimum point of
\bar{f}
.

We call
z\in Z_{0}
a
cylindrical singularity
with respect to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
if every tangent flow is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
. The uniqueness result in Theorem
1.1
, combined with the smooth convergence properties of Ricci flow limit spaces (see Theorem
2.19
), implies the following pointed Cheeger–Gromov convergence:

\displaystyle\left(M,r^{-2}g(-r^{2}),f_{z}(-r^{2}),p_{r}\right)\xrightarrow[r\to 0]{\hskip 8.19447pt\text{pointed Cheeger--Gromov}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f},\bar{p}),

(1.1)

where
f_{z}
is the potential function at
z
(see Definition
2.6
), and
p_{r}
is a minimum point of
f_{z}(-r^{2})
.

However, this convergence has two drawbacks. First, it occurs only in the Cheeger–Gromov sense, which means that the convergence is realized via a sequence of diffeomorphisms from open subsets of
\bar{M}
into
M
, but these diffeomorphisms may be entirely unrelated to one another. Second, the convergence (
1.1
) does not yield any quantitative rate of convergence for the original Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
as it approaches the cylindrical singularity
z
. Thus, in order to obtain more analytical information approaching
z
, one must obtain a stronger result.

To gain more analytic control and resolve these issues, we establish a stronger result—the strong uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow. To this end, we first modify our Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
as follows (see also
[
CMI24
]
).

Let
\phi_{t}
be the family of diffeomorphisms generated by
-\nabla_{g(t)}f_{z}(t)
with
\phi_{-T}=\mathrm{id}
. Define the
modified Ricci flow
with respect to
z
as:

\begin{cases}&g^{z}(s):=e^{s}\phi^{*}_{-e^{-s}}g(-e^{-s}),\\
&f^{z}(s):=\phi^{*}_{-e^{-s}}f_{z}(-e^{-s}),\\
\end{cases}

where
s\in[-\log T,\infty)
. The pair
(g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
evolves by the following system:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\partial_{s}g^{z}(s)=g^{z}-2\mathrm{Ric}(g^{z})-2\nabla^{2}f^{z},\\
&\partial_{s}f^{z}(s)=\frac{n}{2}-\mathrm{R}_{g^{z}}-\Delta f^{z}.\end{cases}

We now state our first main result, which provides a precise convergence estimate for the modified flow near the cylindrical singularity.

Theorem 1.2

(Strong uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow)
.

In the setting above, suppose
z\in Z_{0}
is a cylindrical singularity with respect to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
. Then for any small
\epsilon>0
, there exists a large constant
\bar{s}
such that for any integer
j\geq\bar{s}
, there exists a diffeomorphism
\psi_{j}
from
\Omega^{j}:=\left\{\bar{f}\leq(1-\epsilon)\log j\right\}\subset\bar{M}
onto a subset of
M
satisfying the compatibility condition

\displaystyle\psi_{j+1}=\psi_{j}\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\Omega^{j},

and for all
s\geq j
, the following decay estimate holds on
\Omega^{j}
:

\displaystyle\left[\psi_{j}^{*}g^{z}(s)-\bar{g}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+\left[\psi_{j}^{*}f^{z}(s)-\bar{f}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

(1.2)

Here, for any integer
l\geq 0
, the norm is defined by

\displaystyle[\cdot]_{l}:=\sum_{i=0}^{l}\left|\nabla_{\bar{g}}^{i}(\cdot)\right|_{\bar{g}}.

Roughly speaking, Theorem
1.2
asserts that for sufficiently large
j
, one can construct a diffeomorphism
\psi_{j}
from a region of size approximately
2\sqrt{\log j}
in
\bar{M}
into
M
, such that these diffeomorphisms are compatible on their common domains. Moreover, under each
\psi_{j}
, the modified Ricci flow exhibits a precise decay estimate as (
1.2
). In other words, the modified Ricci flow converges to the cylindrical model
without
modulo any diffeomorphism.

The main technical tool used in the proof of Theorem
1.2
is a Lojasiewicz-type inequality. In his seminal work
[
Loj65
]
, Lojasiewicz proved the following fundamental result: let
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
be a real-analytic function on a domain
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then for any
x_{0}\in U
with
\nabla f(x_{0})=0
, there exist a small neighborhood
W\subset U
and constants
C>0
and
\beta\in[1/2,1)
such that

|f(x)-f(x_{0})|^{\beta}\leq C|\nabla f(x)|,\quad\forall x\in W.

(1.3)

This inequality implies that if
x(t)
is a negative gradient flow of
f
, and
x_{0}
is the limit point of
x(t_{i})
for a sequence
t_{i}\to\infty
, then (
1.3
) can be used to show both convergence of
x(t)\to x_{0}
and a quantitative decay rate depending on the exponent
\beta
(see, for instance,
[
CS14
, Section 3]
).

In his celebrated work
[
Sim83
]
, Leon Simon established an infinite-dimensional version of the Lojasiewicz inequality for analytic functionals on Banach spaces. As an application, he proved a fundamental result on the uniqueness of tangent cones with smooth cross-sections for minimal surfaces—an important theorem mentioned earlier. The key idea in Simon’s approach was to reduce the infinite-dimensional problem to the classical finite-dimensional Lojasiewicz inequality via a Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction.

Simon’s method has since had a profound influence across many areas of analysis and geometry. Variants and extensions of his techniques have been applied in diverse settings, including global convergence results and stability analysis. For instance, see
[
CMI14
,
CS14
,
HM14
,
SW15
,
Krö15
,
CMI14
,
CMI15
,
CMI23
,
Zhu25
,
DO22
,
CS21
]
, among others.

In the context of Ricci flow, the use of Lojasiewicz-type inequalities to prove the uniqueness of tangent flows is not new, as the Ricci flow can be viewed as the gradient flow of Perelman’s
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-functional. In
[
SW15
, Lemma 3.1]
, Sun and Wang established a Lojasiewicz inequality for the
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-functional:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\boldsymbol{\mu}(g,1)-\boldsymbol{\mu}(g_{0},1)\right\rvert^{\beta}\leq C\|\nabla\boldsymbol{\mu}(g,1)\|_{L^{2}},

(1.4)

for some constant
\beta\in[1/2,1)
, where
g_{0}
is a given Ricci shrinker metric on a closed manifold and
g
is a metric nearby. By applying inequality (
1.4
) and following the general strategy, they proved the uniqueness of the tangent flow at a singularity, assuming that one blow-up limit is given by the compact Ricci shrinker
g_{0}
.

It is tempting to directly generalize inequality (
1.4
) to non-compact Ricci shrinkers, but this cannot be done for several reasons. First, in the non-compact setting, a minimizer for Perelman’s
\boldsymbol{\mu}(g,1)
may fail to exist, and even when it does, it may not depend analytically on the metric
g
. Second,
\boldsymbol{\mu}
-functional is inherently a global quantity, and as such, it does not effectively capture localized behavior near a given singularity. Third, when
g_{0}
is a Ricci shrinker on a non-compact manifold, a perturbation
g
that is close to
g_{0}
may only resemble
g_{0}
in a large but bounded region, without any control at infinity.

To overcome these difficulties, we use the
pointed
\mathcal{W}
-entropy
—first introduced in
[
Per02
, Section 5]
and studied systematically in
[
HN14
]
—a localized version of Perelman’s
\mathcal{W}
-functional. Specifically, if we write the conjugate heat kernel at
z\in Z_{0}
by
\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;t}=(4\pi|t|)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-f_{z}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
, then the pointed
\mathcal{W}
-entropy at
z
is defined by

\mathcal{W}_{z}(\tau):=\int_{M}\tau(2\Delta f_{z}-|\nabla f_{z}|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f_{z}-n\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{z;-\tau}.

We are now ready to state the Lojasiewicz inequality for the pointed
\mathcal{W}
-entropy:

Theorem 1.3

(Lojasiewicz inequality)
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be the completion of a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. For any cylindrical singularity
z\in Z_{0}
with respect to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
, the following property holds.

For any
\beta\in(0,3/4)
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,Y)
such that for all sufficiently small
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{z}(\tau)-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq C\left(\mathcal{W}_{z}(\tau/2)-\mathcal{W}_{z}(2\tau)\right)^{\beta}.

(1.5)

Inequality (
1.5
) is often referred to as a discrete Lojasiewicz inequality, and can be seen as a discretized variant of the classical version (
1.3
). Unlike the classical setting in
[
Sim83
]
, we do not rely on Simon’s argument, as our model space is non-compact. Instead, we provide a direct proof of (
1.5
). Similar forms of discrete Lojasiewicz inequalities have appeared in the context of mean curvature flow, particularly in the work of Colding and Minicozzi
[
CMI15
,
CMI23
]
, where they established analogous results for cylindrical singularities, using entropy functionals developed in
[
CMI12
]
.

In our setting, this inequality yields a natural summability result for the entropy difference, which plays an important role in quantifying the convergence toward cylindrical models. To formulate this precisely, we introduce the following notion:

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
such that
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is the metric completion of
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)}
with respect to
d^{*}
. A point
z\in Z
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}\geq-0.98T
and

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k},d_{\mathcal{C}},p^{*},\mathfrak{t})\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}],

where the definition of
\epsilon
-closeness can be found in Definition
2.25
.

We now state a direct consequence of Theorem
1.3
, whose proof will be given in Corollary
6.28
:

Corollary 1.4
.

Given constants
\epsilon>0
,
\alpha\in(1/4,1)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\alpha)
, the following holds.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Fix
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in M\times[-T,0)
and constants
s_{2}>s_{1}>0
. If

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(s_{1})-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(s_{2})\right|<\delta,

and, for any
s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
,
x_{0}^{*}
is
(k,\delta,\sqrt{s})
-cylindrical, then

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq s_{2}}\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j-1})\right|^{\alpha}<\epsilon.

(1.6)

In our forthcoming work
[
FL25b
]
, we extend Theorem
1.3
and Corollary
1.4
to noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces. The summability inequality (
1.6
) with
\alpha=1/2
is a key analytic ingredient in our analysis of cylindrical singularities. Specifically, we prove in
[
FL25b
]
that, for any noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space, the subset of the singular set consisting of points at which some tangent flow is
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
or a quotient thereof is
parabolic
k
-rectifiable
with respect to the spacetime distance introduced in
[
FL25a
]
.

Outline of the Proof of Theorem
1.3

The proof of Theorem
1.3
involves several technical challenges and key analytic ingredients. We provide an overview of the main steps below.

(A)
Variation of the
\mathcal{W}
-functional under global perturbations of the cylinder

We begin by considering the model cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{-1}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})
, along with a global perturbation
(g,f)
, such that

\displaystyle h:=g-\bar{g},\quad\chi:=f-\bar{f},\quad\text{with}\quad\|h\|_{C^{2}}+\|\chi\|_{C^{2}}\ll 1.

Following the approach in
[
CMI25
]
and
[
LZ23
]
, we apply the decomposition:

\displaystyle h=ug_{S^{n-k}}+\zeta,\quad\chi=\frac{n-k}{2}u+q,

where
ug_{S^{n-k}}
denotes the projection of
h
onto
\mathcal{K}_{0}g_{S^{n-k}}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{0}
is the space of quadratic Hermite polynomials (see Definition
2.4
).

In Section
4
(see Theorem
4.17
), we establish the following variational inequality for the
\mathcal{W}
-functional:

\left|\mathcal{W}(g,f)-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq C\left(\rVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}^{3}+\rVert\mathbf{\Phi}(g,f)\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\rVert\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}}h\rVert_{W^{1,2}}^{2}+|\mathcal{B}(h,\chi)|^{2}+\left|\mathcal{V}(g,f)-1\right|\right).

(1.7)

Here:

•

\mathcal{W}(g,f)
is Perelman’s
\mathcal{W}
-functional at scale
1
(see Definition
2.2
),

•

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(g,f):=\frac{g}{2}-\mathrm{Ric}(g)-\nabla^{2}f
is the Ricci shrinker operator,

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(g,f)=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{\bar{M}}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}
is the weighted volume,

•

\mathcal{B}(h,\chi)
is the center of mass vector (see (
4.3
)), and

•

all
L^{2}
-norms are taken with respect to the weighted measure
\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\bar{f}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{g}}
.

Inequality (
1.7
) is derived via a Taylor expansion of the
\mathcal{W}
-functional on the cylinder, combined with a quadratic rigidity inequality (see Proposition
4.3
) from
[
CMI25
]
.

Let

\displaystyle g(s):=\bar{g}+sh,\quad f(s):=\bar{f}+s\chi,\quad W(s):=\mathcal{W}(g(s),f(s)).

Then the Taylor expansion yields:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}(g,f)-\Theta_{n-k}\right|=\left|W(1)-W(0)\right|\leq\left|W^{\prime}(0)+\frac{1}{2}W^{\prime\prime}(0)\right|+\frac{1}{6}\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\left|W^{\prime\prime\prime}(s)\right|.

(1.8)

By performing detailed calculations for
W^{\prime}
,
W^{\prime\prime}
, and
W^{\prime\prime\prime}
, and carefully applying the rigidity inequality, we control each term on the right-hand side of (
1.8
), which ultimately yields inequality (
1.7
).

(B)
Characterization of almost cylindrical regions in the Ricci flow

Next, we consider a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M,(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
and a based point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
such that
[t_{0}-2r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
. Then we consider the conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{t}=\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t}
with potential function
f=f_{x_{0}^{*}}
. For simplicity, we assume
t_{0}=0
,
r=1
, and
\tau=-t
.

For the weighted Riemannian manifold
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
, the geometry may not arise from a global perturbation of the cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{-1}
, even if it is locally close to one. Therefore, to detect and work with such almost cylindrical regions, we introduce a set of radius functions that quantify the size of regions in
M
where the geometry closely approximates that of a cylinder. We define
\bar{b}:=2\sqrt{\bar{f}}
and introduce four key radii:

Definition 1.5
.

For small positive constants
\sigma
and
\delta
, and the weighted Riemannian manifold
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
with
\Phi=\mathbf{\Phi}(g(-1),f(-1))
:

(
\textbf{r}_{A}
-radius)
.
\textbf{r}_{A}
is defined as the largest number
L
such that there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{A}
from
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}\subset\bar{M}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{A}^{*}g(-1)\right]_{5}+\left[\bar{f}-\varphi_{A}^{*}f(-1)\right]_{5}\leq e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}.

(1.9)

(
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
-radius)
.
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
is defined as the largest
L
such that there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{B}
from
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}\subset\bar{M}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g(-1)\right]_{0}+\left[\bar{f}-\varphi_{B}^{*}f(-1)\right]_{0}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}},

(1.10)

and

\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L\}}\left|\varphi_{B}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4-\sigma}}.

(1.11)

Furthermore, for all
l\in[1,10^{10}n\sigma^{-1}]
, the
C^{l}
-norms of
\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g(-1)
and
\bar{f}-\varphi_{B}^{*}f(-1)
are bounded by
1
.

(
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
-radius)
.
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
is defined as the largest
L
such that there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{C}
from
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
of
\bar{M}
onto a subset of
M
such that
f\left(\varphi_{C}(\bar{p}),-1\right)\leq n
and

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{C}^{*}g(-1)\right]_{2}\leq\delta.

(
\mathbf{r}_{E}
-radius)
. The
entropy radius

\mathbf{r}_{E}
is defined as

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{\mathbf{r}^{2}_{E}}{4}\right):=\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/2)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2).

The radius functions
\textbf{r}_{A}
and
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
are closely related to the conditions
(\star_{L})
and
(\dagger_{L})
introduced in
[
LZ23
, Section 5]
; see also similar conditions in
[
CMI25
, Section 7]
. Unlike in
[
LZ23
]
, however, we incorporate an additional integral condition (
1.11
) in the definition of
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
. In the setting of
[
LZ23
]
, this condition is trivially satisfied. The
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
-radius is essentially weaker than both
\textbf{r}_{A}
and
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
, and can be viewed as an analog of the graphical radius introduced in
[
CMI15
, Section 0.6]
. In what follows, we will compare the three radii—
\textbf{r}_{A}
,
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
, and
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
—within the entropy radius
\mathbf{r}_{E}
.

(I) Given
\sigma>0
, we have
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}-2
if
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
is sufficiently large.

The proof of this statement is given in Proposition
5.2
, which applies to general weighted Riemannian manifolds. In the special case where
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
is a Ricci shrinker, the same conclusion was established in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.2]
, building on techniques developed in
[
CMI25
, Section 3]
. Broadly speaking, the argument proceeds by applying a suitable cutoff to the pair
\left(\varphi_{B}^{*}g(-1)-\bar{g},\varphi_{B}^{*}f(-1)-\bar{f}\right)
, followed by the construction of a modified diffeomorphism that yields improved estimates as a consequence of the rigidity inequality. Although
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
is not assumed to be a Ricci shrinker in our setting, the assumption (
1.11
) is sufficient to obtain the necessary estimates.

From the above argument, one can derive the following estimate (see Theorem
5.4
), which is based on (
1.7
) and the remainder estimate given in Proposition
3.1
:

\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1)-\Theta_{n-k}\right|\leq C(n,Y)\exp\left(-\frac{3\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{2}}{16}\right).

(1.12)

To apply (
1.7
), it is necessary to consider a cutoff of the pair
\left(\varphi_{B}^{*}g(-1)-\bar{g},\varphi_{B}^{*}f(-1)-\bar{f}\right)
. Accordingly, Proposition
3.1
is used to show that the contribution to the
\mathcal{W}
-functional from outside a compact set of size
L
decays like
e^{-L^{2}/4}
.

(II) Suppose
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}<(1-\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}
. If
\delta
is sufficiently small and
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
is sufficiently large, then the pair
\left(\varphi_{C}^{*}g(-1),\varphi_{C}^{*}f(-1)\right)
is close to a Ricci shrinker in the sense that

\left[\frac{\varphi_{C}^{*}g(-1)}{2}-\varphi_{C}^{*}\nabla^{2}f(-1)-\mathrm{Ric}\left(\varphi_{C}^{*}g(-1)\right)\right]_{100}\leq C(n,Y,\sigma)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}-\frac{\bar{L}^{2}}{8(1-\sigma)}},

(1.13)

on
\{\bar{b}\leq\bar{L}\}
, where
\bar{L}=\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}-\delta^{-1}
.

Here, the constant
100
is not essential and can be replaced by any sufficiently large constant, provided the corresponding parameters are adjusted accordingly. The proof of (
1.13
) will be given in Proposition
6.13
. This estimate shows that, as long as
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
does not exceed
\mathbf{r}_{E}
, the Ricci shrinker operator
\Phi
admits a pointwise bound within
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
-radius. This is somewhat surprising, since the definition of the entropy radius a priori only yields a spacetime
L^{2}
control on
\Phi
.

Through a series of delicate estimates, we demonstrate that a pointwise bound is nonetheless achievable. A key step is the observation that
\varphi_{C}^{*}f(-1)
is nearly equal to
\bar{f}
, up to a multiplicative factor of
1\pm\epsilon
(see Proposition
6.3
), the proof of which relies on an optimal heat kernel estimate (see Theorem
2.13
). Then, by introducing an auxiliary function (see Proposition
6.7
), we establish an
L^{2}
estimate on the time slice
t=-1
, which ultimately leads to the desired pointwise bound in (
1.13
).

(III) Suppose
\textbf{r}_{A}<(1-\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}
. Then for any sufficiently large constant
D>1
, we have
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}\geq\textbf{r}_{A}+D
, provided
\textbf{r}_{A}
is large enough. More precisely, the map
\varphi_{A}
can be extended to a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}_{A}
defined on the region
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}+D\}
, which satisfies the conditions required in the definition of
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
.

Intuitively, this means that the domain of
\varphi_{A}
can be extended to a larger region, at the expense of obtaining weaker estimates. This result will be established in Theorem
6.14
, one of the most technically involved parts of the paper. The key idea is to analyze the geometry near the boundary of the region where
\varphi_{A}
is defined and to show that the pullback metric
\varphi_{A}^{*}g(-1)
is locally almost splitting. The approximate splitting direction is almost generated by
\varphi_{A}^{*}\nabla f(-1)
, after an appropriate rescaling.

To realize this, we employ a limiting argument based on the structure theory of Ricci flow limit spaces developed in
[
FL25a
]
. The resulting limit space is the standard
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\times S^{n-k}
, where the first factor corresponds to the splitting direction. Following the gradient flow of this approximate splitting direction, we are able to extend
\varphi_{A}
to a larger region while maintaining the necessary control. A similar idea was used in
[
LW23
]
in the context of Ricci shrinkers to identify a splitting direction.

We remark that this extension argument differs from those used in
[
CMI25
, Theorem 7.1]
and
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
, both of which rely heavily on pseudolocality and the self-similarity of Ricci shrinkers. Although the pseudolocality theorem remains applicable in our setting, the absence of self-similarity prevents us from deriving spatial estimates based on estimates at later times.

(IV) Suppose
\textbf{r}_{A}<(1-2\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}
. Then
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\geq\textbf{r}_{A}+D
, provided that
\textbf{r}_{A}
is sufficiently large, where
D
is a fixed large constant.

This can be viewed as another extension step and will be established in Theorem
6.18
. The overall strategy follows a similar approach to that in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
(see also
[
CMI25
, Theorem 7.1]
). One key difference is that the additional neck region obtained in statement (III) is smaller than that in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
, where the neck has size
\epsilon\textbf{r}_{A}
. Nevertheless, the same gluing argument can be carried out within this narrower neck region.

Another distinction lies in the geometric setting:
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
deals with a genuine Ricci shrinker, whereas here we do not assume that
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
is a Ricci shrinker. However, this poses no essential difficulty, as (
1.13
) implies that
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
is an almost Ricci shrinker in a very strong sense—sufficient for the estimates required in our argument.

Thus, by applying statements (I) and (IV) iteratively, one obtains the estimate

\displaystyle\min\{\mathbf{r}_{A},\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\}\geq(1-3\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E},

(1.14)

provided that
\textbf{r}_{A}
or
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
is sufficiently large. When combined with (
1.12
), this leads to the Lojasiewicz inequality (
1.5
) stated in Theorem
1.3
. ∎

Based on Theorem
1.3
, we sketch the proof of how it leads to a strong uniqueness result of Theorem
1.2
.

Outline of the Proof of Theorem
1.2

In the setting of Theorem
1.2
, let
\textbf{r}_{A}(s)
,
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)
, and
\mathbf{r}_{E}(s)
denote the corresponding radius functions associated with the triple
(M,g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
. By Theorem
1.3
, we have
\mathbf{r}_{E}(s)\geq\sqrt{(12-\epsilon)\log s}
, provided that
s
is sufficiently large. Then, applying (
1.14
), we obtain

\displaystyle\min\{\textbf{r}_{A}(s),\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)\}\geq\sqrt{(12-\epsilon)\log s}.

(1.15)

Let
\varphi_{s}
denote the diffeomorphism that appears in the definition of
\textbf{r}_{A}(s)
. Based on estimates (
1.13
) and (
1.15
), one can show that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a sufficiently large constant
\bar{s}
such that for all
s_{0}\geq\bar{s}
, the map
\varphi_{s_{0}}
defines a diffeomorphism from the region
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
onto a subset of
M
, and for all
s\geq s_{0}
, the following estimate holds on
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
:

\displaystyle\left[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}g^{z}(s)-\bar{g}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+\left[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}f^{z}(s)-\bar{f}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

(1.16)

Note that the estimate (
1.16
) remains valid if we replace
s_{0}
by
s
, but the key point here is that it also holds with a fixed diffeomorphism
\varphi_{s_{0}}
, independent of
s\geq s_{0}
. The full details will be presented in Theorem
7.2
. Based on (
1.16
), one can construct the sequence of diffeomorphisms
\psi_{j}
in Theorem
1.2
inductively, ensuring they are compatible on overlapping regions. ∎

The main results of this paper—including the strong uniqueness theorem (Theorem
1.2
) and the Lojasiewicz inequality (Theorem
1.3
)—can be extended to the setting of quotient cylinders (see Section
8
). While the overall structure of the proofs remains similar, it is necessary to modify the definitions of
\textbf{r}_{A}
,
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
, and
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}
to suit the quotient cylinder context; see Definitions
8.1
and
8.4
.

More precisely, rather than considering a diffeomorphism from the model space
\bar{M}/\Gamma
—where
\Gamma
is a finite group acting freely on
\bar{M}
—into
M
, we instead consider a smooth covering map of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain in
\bar{M}
onto a subset of
M
. This map is constructed so that the pullbacks of
(g(-1),f(-1))
satisfy estimates analogous to (
1.9
) or (
1.10
). A similar formulation was used in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.5]
.

Following this modification, we can establish a Lojasiewicz inequality for quotient cylindrical singularities (see Theorem
8.9
). Furthermore, the strong uniqueness of the associated tangent flow can also be proved (see Theorem
8.15
). Likewise, an analog of Corollary
1.4
can be obtained in this setting (see Corollary
8.13
).

In addition, one may consider ancient solutions to Ricci flow with bounded entropy, where a tangent flow at infinity is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
or its finite quotient. In this context, similar strong uniqueness theorems can be established by working with the appropriately modified Ricci flow (see (
7.12
)) and applying the Lojasiewicz inequality. See Theorems
7.10
and
8.19
for the precise statements.

Organization of the Paper

The paper is organized as follows.

•

Section
2
introduces the necessary definitions and basic properties of weighted Riemannian manifolds. We also present fundamental conventions and results for closed Ricci flows that will be used throughout the paper. In addition, we review key results on Ricci flow limit spaces from
[
FL25a
]
, introduce our model spaces, and prove Theorem
1.1
.

•

Section
3
establishes the remainder estimate for integrals.

•

Section
4
derives the variational inequality for the
\mathcal{W}
-functional near the cylinder.

•

Section
5
defines various radius functions and proves statement (I) as well as the estimate (
1.12
).

•

Section
6
, which forms the core of the paper, contains the proofs of statements (II), (III), and (IV), thereby completing the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

•

Section
7
proves Theorem
1.2
, building on the results of Theorem
1.3
.

•

Section
8
extends the above results to the setting of quotient cylinders.

•

Finally, we include a list of notations for reference.

Yu Li is supported by YSBR-001, NSFC-12201597, NSFC-12522105 and research funds from the University of Science and Technology of China and the Chinese Academy of Sciences.

2
Preliminaries

Weighted Riemannian manifolds

An
n
-dimensional
weighted Riemannian manifold

(M^{n},g,f)
is a complete Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g)
coupled with a smooth function
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
. For a weighted Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g,f)
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(g,f):=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}\int_{M}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}.

Here,
\mathrm{d}V_{g}
denotes the volume form of
(M,g)
. For simplicity, we set
\mathrm{d}V_{f}:=(4\pi)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}
. We say
(M^{n},g,f)
is
normalized
if it satisfies the following normalization:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(g,f)=1.

(2.1)

Example 2.1

(Model space: weighted cylinders)
.

For any
n\geq 3
and
m\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1}:=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{n-m}\times S^{m},g_{E}\times g_{S^{m}},\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{m}{2}+\Theta_{m}\right),

where
g_{E}
is the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n-m}
, and
g_{S^{m}}
is the round metric on
S^{m}
such that
\mathrm{Ric}(g_{S^{m}})=g_{S^{m}}/2
. The vector
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-m})
denotes the standard coordinate function on
\mathbb{R}^{n-m}
. The constant
\Theta_{m}
is chosen to ensure that the normalization condition (
2.1
) is satisfied. More explicitly, it follows from a direct calculation that

\displaystyle\Theta_{m}=\log\left(\frac{2^{1-\frac{m}{2}}(m-1)^{\frac{m}{2}}e^{-\frac{m}{2}}\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{m+1}{2}\right)}\right).

(2.2)

Definition 2.2
.

For a weighted Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g,f)
, we define:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(g,f):=

\displaystyle\frac{g}{2}-\mathrm{Ric}(g)-\nabla^{2}f,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(g,f):=

\displaystyle\int_{M}2\Delta f-|\nabla{f}|^{2}+\mathrm{R}+f-n\,\mathrm{d}V_{f}.

It is clear that
\mathbf{\Phi}(g,f)=0
implies that the weighted Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g,f)
is given by a Ricci shrinker. On the other hand,
\mathcal{W}(g,f)
is nothing but Perelman’s
\mathcal{W}
-functional
\mathcal{W}(g,f,1)
(see
[
Per02
]
). For the cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})
as in Example
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g},\bar{f})=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{W}(\bar{g},\bar{f})=\Theta_{m}.

In other words,
\Theta_{m}
denotes the entropy of
(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})
as a Ricci shrinker. For more information regarding the entropy of Ricci shrinkers, we refer readers to
[
LW20
, Section 5]
.

For later applications, we have the following definition.

Definition 2.3
.

Given a weighted Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g,f)
, let
T^{r,s}M
denote the space of
(r,s)
-tensors on
M
, and let
S^{2}(M)
denote the space of symmetric
(0,2)
-tensors. We then define the following:

(i)

The
L^{2}
-norm for
(r,s)
-tensors is defined with respect to
\mathrm{d}V_{f}
, i.e., for any
T\in L^{2}(T^{r,s}M)
,
\rVert T\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\int_{M}|T|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f}
. The Sobolev spaces
W^{k,2}(T^{r,s}M)
are defined with respect to
\mathrm{d}V_{f}
as well.

(ii)

The weighted divergence operator
\mathrm{div}_{f}:C^{\infty}(T^{r,s}M)\to C^{\infty}(T^{r,s-1}M)
is defined as

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}_{f}T)^{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{s-1}}:=\nabla_{i}T^{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}_{i,j_{1},\cdots,j_{s-1}}-T^{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}_{i,j_{1},\cdots,j_{s-1}}f_{i},

for any
T\in C^{\infty}(T^{r,s}M)
. In particular, when
f
is constant, then
\mathrm{div}_{f}
reduces to the standard divergence operator, which we denote by
\mathrm{div}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{div}_{f}^{*}
the
L^{2}
-adjoint of
\mathrm{div}_{f}
.

(iii)

For a tensor field
T
on
M
, we set

\displaystyle[T]_{l}:=\sum_{k=0}^{l}\left|\nabla^{k}T\right|.

(2.3)

For any two tensor fields
T_{1},T_{2}
on
M
, we use the notation
T_{1}*T_{2}
to denote any tensor field that is a real linear combination of terms obtained from
T_{1}\otimes T_{2}
by contracting indices using the metric—i.e., by converting any number of
T^{*}M
components to
TM
components or vice versa.

(iv)

The weighted Laplacian
\Delta_{f}
is defined as
\Delta_{f}:=\Delta-\langle\nabla\cdot,\nabla f\rangle
on
C^{\infty}(T^{r,s}M)
. Denote
\mathcal{L}
to be the stability operator
\Delta_{f}+2\mathrm{Rm}
on
C^{\infty}(S^{2}(M))
, i.e., for any such tensor
h\in C^{\infty}(S^{2}(M))
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(h)_{ij}:=\Delta_{f}h_{ij}+2\mathrm{Rm}_{likj}h_{lk}.

For weighted cylinders, we define the following space.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})
denote the weighted cylinder as described in Example
2.1
. We define
\mathcal{K}_{0}
to be the linear space of quadratic Hermite polynomials of the form

\displaystyle v=a_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}-2\mathrm{Tr}(a)

where
a=(a_{ij})
is a symmetric matrix.

On the cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1}
, the
L^{2}
-kernel of the stability operator
\mathcal{L}
in
\ker(\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}})
is
\mathcal{K}_{0}g_{S^{m}}
; see
[
CMI25
, Section 6]
and
[
LZ23
, Proposition 4.2]
.

We conclude this subsection with the following weighted Bianchi identity, which is well-known to experts.

Lemma 2.5
.

For any weighted Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},g,f)
and constant
\tau>0
, the following identity holds:

\nabla\left(\tau(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f-n\right)=2\mathrm{div}_{f}\left(\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}(\tau f)-\frac{g}{2}\right).

Proof.

By the traced Bianchi identity
\text{div}(\mathrm{Ric})=\nabla\mathrm{R}/2
and the identity
\text{div}(\nabla^{2}f)=\nabla\Delta f+\mathrm{Ric}(\nabla f)
, we have

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{div}_{f}\left(\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}(\tau f)-\frac{g}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2\text{div}(\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\tau\nabla^{2}f)-2(\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\tau\nabla^{2}f-\frac{g}{2})(\nabla f)

\displaystyle=\tau\nabla\mathrm{R}+2\tau\nabla\Delta f-2\tau\nabla^{2}f(\nabla f)+\nabla f=\nabla\left(\tau(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f-n\right).

∎

Basic conventions and results for Ricci flows

Throughout this paper, we consider a closed Ricci flow solution
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
, where
M
is an
n
-dimensional closed manifold,
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is a closed interval, and
(g(t))_{t\in I}
is a family of smooth metrics on
M
satisfying for all
t\in I
the Ricci flow equation:

\partial_{t}g(t)=-2\mathrm{Ric}(g(t)).

Following notations in
[
FL25a
]
, we use
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
to denote a spacetime point
x^{*}\in M\times I
, and define
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})
to be its time component. We denote by
d_{t}
the distance function and by
\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
the volume form induced by the metric
g(t)
. For
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}
, we write
B_{t}(x,r)
for the geodesic ball centered at
x
with radius
r
at time
t
. The Riemannian curvature, Ricci curvature, and scalar curvature are denoted by
\mathrm{Rm}
,
\mathrm{Ric}
, and
\mathrm{R}
, respectively, with the time parameter omitted when there is no ambiguity. Throughout this paper, we use
\Psi(\epsilon)
to denote a function satisfying
\Psi(\epsilon)\to 0
if
\epsilon\to 0
.

For the smooth closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
, we set
K(x,t;y,s)
to be the heat kernel, which is determined by:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\square K(\cdot,\cdot;y,s)=0,\\
&\square^{*}K(x,t;\cdot,\cdot)=0,\\
&\lim_{t\searrow s}K(\cdot,t;y,s)=\delta_{y},\\
&\lim_{s\nearrow t}K(x,t;\cdot,s)=\delta_{x}.\end{cases}

where
\square:=\partial_{t}-\Delta
and
\square^{*}:=-\partial_{t}-\Delta+\mathrm{R}
.

Definition 2.6
.

The
conjugate heat kernel measure

\nu_{x^{*};s}
based at
x^{*}=(x,t)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x,t;s}:=K(x,t;\cdot,s)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)}.

It is clear that
\nu_{x^{*};s}
is a probability measure on
M
. If we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};s}=(4\pi(t-s))^{-n/2}e^{-f_{x^{*}}(\cdot,s)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)},

then the function
f_{x^{*}}
is called the
potential function
at
x^{*}
which satisfies:

-\partial_{s}f_{x^{*}}=\Delta f_{x^{*}}-|\nabla f_{x^{*}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R}-\frac{n}{2(t-s)}.

(2.4)

Next, we recall the definitions of the Nash entropy and the
\mathcal{W}
-entropy based at a spacetime point
x^{*}
.

Definition 2.7
.

The
Nash entropy
based at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
is defined by

\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau):=\int_{M}f_{x^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}-\frac{n}{2},

for
\tau>0
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau\in I
, where
f_{x^{*}}
is the potential function at
x^{*}
.
Moreover, the
\mathcal{W}
-entropy based at
(x,t)
is defined by

\mathcal{W}_{x^{*}}(\tau):=\int_{M}\tau(2\Delta f_{x^{*}}-|\nabla f_{x^{*}}|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f_{x^{*}}-n\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}.

The following proposition gives basic properties of Nash entropy (see
[
Bam20a
, Section 5]
):

Proposition 2.8
.

For any
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
with
\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau\in I
and
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau)\geq R_{\min}
, we have the following inequalities:

(i)

\displaystyle-\frac{n}{2\tau}+R_{\min}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\leq 0
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\left(\tau\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\right)=\mathcal{W}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\leq 0
;

(iii)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}\left(\tau\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\right)=-2\tau\int_{M}\left\lvert\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}f_{x^{*}}-\frac{1}{2\tau}g\right\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*};\mathfrak{t}(x^{*})-\tau}\leq 0
.

As in
[
FL25a
]
, we have the following definition.

Definition 2.9
.

A closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
is said to have entropy bounded below by
-Y
at
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
if

\displaystyle\inf_{\tau>0}\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)\geq-Y,

(2.5)

where the infimum is taken over all
\tau>0
whenever the Nash entropy
\mathcal{N}_{x^{*}}(\tau)
is well-defined.

Moreover, we say the Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
if (
2.5
) holds for all
x^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
.

Next, we recall the following definition.

Definition 2.10
.

For
x^{*}=(x,t)\in\mathcal{X}
, the curvature radius
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}
is defined as the supremum over all
r>0
such that
|\mathrm{Rm}|\leq r^{-2}
on the parabolic ball
B_{t}(x,r)\times[t-r^{2},t+r^{2}]\cap I
.

Now we recall the following definition of
H
-center from
[
Bam20a
, Definition 3.10]
.

Definition 2.11
.

A point
(z,t)\in\mathcal{X}
is called an
H
-center
of
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
for a constant
H>0
if
t\in I
,
t<\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}_{t}(\delta_{z},\nu_{x_{0}^{*};t})\leq H(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t).

Here,
\mathrm{Var}_{t}
denotes the variance between two probability measures with respect to
g(t)
; see
[
FL25a
, Definition 2.1]
. Note that by
[
Bam20a
, Corollary 3.8]
, an
H_{n}
-center, where
H_{n}:=(n-1)\pi^{2}/4+2
, must exist for any
t<\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})
.

We have the following result from
[
Bam20a
, Propositions 3.12, 3.13]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Any two
H
-centers
(z_{1},t)
and
(z_{2},t)
of
x_{0}^{*}
satisfy
d_{t}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq 2\sqrt{H(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t)}
. Moreover, if
(z,t)
is an
H
-center of
x_{0}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}
, then for any
L>0
, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}\left(B_{t}\left(z,\sqrt{LH(\mathfrak{t}(x_{0}^{*})-t)}\right)\right)\geq 1-L^{-1}.

The following estimates are proved in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 2.15]
, which have improved the corresponding results in
[
Bam20a
]
and will play important roles later.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with
[t,t_{0}]\subset I
. Then for any
\epsilon>0,L>0
and
(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
, the following statements hold.

(i)

We have

\displaystyle\nu_{x_{0},t_{0};t}\left(M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)\right)\leq C(n,\epsilon)\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{(4+\epsilon)(t_{0}-t)}\right).

(ii)

If
\mathrm{R}(\cdot,t)\geq R_{\min}
, then for any
(y,t)\in\mathcal{X}
,

K(x_{0},t_{0};y,t)\leq\frac{C\left(n,R_{\min}(t_{0}-t),\epsilon\right)}{(t-s)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{d_{t}^{2}(z,y)}{(4+\epsilon)(t_{0}-t)}-\mathcal{N}_{x_{0},t_{0}}(t_{0}-t)\right).

Here,
(z,t)
is any
H_{n}
-center of
(x_{0},t_{0})
.

We also need the following two-sided pseudolocality from
[
Per02
, Theorem 10.1]
and
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 2.47]
:

Theorem 2.14

(Two-sided pseudolocality theorem)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow. For any
\alpha>0
, there is an
\epsilon(n,\alpha)>0
such that the following holds.

Given
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and
r>0
with
[t_{0}-r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
, if
|B_{t_{0}}(x_{0},r)|_{t_{0}}\geq\alpha r^{n}
and
|\mathrm{Rm}|\leq(\alpha r)^{-2}
on
B_{t_{0}}(x_{0},r)
, then

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{Rm}}(x_{0}^{*})\geq\epsilon r.

Ricci flow limit spaces

In this subsection, we review the construction of noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces and their key properties, as developed in
[
FL25a
]
.

As in
[
FL25a
]
, we consider the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
of closed Ricci flows defined as follows:

Definition 2.15

(Moduli space)
.

For fixed constants
T\in(0,+\infty]
and
Y>0
, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
consists of all
n
-dimensional closed Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}
satisfying

(i)

\mathcal{X}
is defined on
\mathbb{I}^{++}:=[-T,0]
.

(ii)

\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
(cf. Definition
2.9
).

In addition, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}^{+}:=[-0.99T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}:=[-0.98T,0],\quad\mathbb{I}^{-}:=(-0.98T,0].

As noted in
[
FL25a
]
, these intervals can generally be chosen as
[-(1-\sigma)T,0]
,
[-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
, and
(-(1-2\sigma)T,0]
, respectively, where
\sigma>0
is an arbitrarily small parameter. For simplicity, we fix
\sigma=1/100
in the present setting.

For any
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
, we have the following definition of the spacetime distance on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
:

Definition 2.16
.

For any
x^{*}=(x,t),y^{*}=(y,s)\in M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
with
s\leq t
, we define

\displaystyle d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\inf_{r\in[\sqrt{t-s},\sqrt{t+0.99T})}\left\{r\mid d_{W_{1}}^{t-r^{2}}(\nu_{x^{*};t-r^{2}},\nu_{y^{*};t-r^{2}})\leq\epsilon_{0}r\right\}.

If no such
r
exists, we define
d^{*}(x^{*},y^{*}):=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}d_{W_{1}}^{-0.99T}(\nu_{x^{*};-0.99T},\nu_{y^{*};-0.99T})
.

Here,
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
is a small constant depending on
n
and
Y
(see
[
FL25a
, Definition 3.3]
). By
[
FL25a
, Lemma 3.7]
,
d^{*}
defines a distance function on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
, which coincides with the standard topology on
M\times\mathbb{I}^{+}
(see
[
FL25a
, Corollary 3.11]
).

The following weak compactness theorem is proved in
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Theorem 2.17

(Weak compactness)
.

Given any sequence
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M_{i}^{n},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{I}^{++}}\}\in\mathcal{M}(n,Y,T)
with base points
p_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}

(
when
T=+\infty
, we additionally assume
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(p_{i}^{*})>-\infty
)
, by taking a subsequence if necessary, we obtain the pointed Gromov–Hausdorff convergence

\displaystyle(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},d^{*}_{i},p_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z,d_{Z},p_{\infty},\mathfrak{t}),

where
d^{*}_{i}
denotes the restriction of the
d^{*}
-distance on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
is the standard time-function on
M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is a complete, separable, locally compact metric space coupled with a
2
-Hölder continuous time-function
\mathfrak{t}:Z\to\mathbb{I}
.

The limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is referred to as a
noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
over
\mathbb{I}
. Locally, the limit space is closely related to the
\mathbb{F}
-limit developed by Bamler in
[
Bam23
]
. More precisely, for any
z\in Z
, we take a sequence of points
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converging to
z
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense. By the theory of
\mathbb{F}
-convergence (see
[
Bam23
]
), there exists a correspondence
\mathfrak{C}
such that

(\mathcal{X}^{i},(\nu_{z_{i}^{*};t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}^{i}(z_{i}^{*})]})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{C}\hskip 8.19447pt}(\mathcal{X}^{z},(\nu_{z;t})_{t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]})

(2.6)

such that the metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is future continuous for all
t\in[-T,\mathfrak{t}(z)]
, except possibly at
t=-0.99T
, where we require that the convergence (
2.6
) is uniform. The metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
is referred to as the
metric flow associated with
z
with time-function denoted by
\mathfrak{t}^{z}
. On
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
, one can define a spacetime function
d_{z}^{*}
as Definition
2.16
. In general,
d_{z}^{*}
is only a pseudo-distance on
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}^{+}}
. However, by passing to the corresponding quotient space
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
, one obtains an isometric embedding into the limit space
Z
(cf.
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.4]
):

Theorem 2.18
.

For any
z\in Z
, there exists an isometric embedding

\displaystyle\iota_{z}:(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}},d^{*}_{z})\longrightarrow(Z,d_{Z})

such that
\iota_{z}(z)=z
and
\mathfrak{t}\circ\iota_{z}=\mathfrak{t}^{z}
, where
\mathfrak{t}^{z}
is the time-function on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
. Moreover, for any
y_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{\mathbb{I}}
and
y_{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
,
y_{i}^{*}
converge to
y_{\infty}
within
\mathfrak{C}
if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\to\iota_{z}(\tilde{y}_{\infty})
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense, where
\tilde{y}_{\infty}
is the quotient of
y_{\infty}
from
\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}^{z}_{\mathbb{I}}}
.

The limit space
Z
contains a regular part
\mathcal{R}
, which is a dense open subset of
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
(see
[
FL25a
, Corollary 5.7]
) and carries the structure of a Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. On this regular part, the convergence described in Theorem
2.17
is smooth, in the following sense (cf.
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.5]
):

Theorem 2.19

(Smooth convergence)
.

There exists an increasing sequence
U_{1}\subset U_{2}\subset\ldots\subset\mathcal{R}
of open subsets with
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}U_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, open subsets
V_{i}\subset M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
, time-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}:U_{i}\to V_{i}
and a sequence
\epsilon_{i}\to 0
such that the following holds:

(a)

We have

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}g^{i}-g^{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

\displaystyle\|\phi_{i}^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
g^{i}
is the spacetime metric induced by
g_{i}(t)
, and
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}_{i}}
is the standard time vector field induced by
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
.

(b)

Let
y\in\mathcal{R}
and
y_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
. Then
y_{i}^{*}\to y
in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense if and only if
y_{i}^{*}\in V_{i}
for large
i
and
\phi_{i}^{-1}(y_{i}^{*})\to y
in
\mathcal{R}
.

(c)

For
U_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x,y)\in U_{i}\times U_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}(x)>\mathfrak{t}(y)+\epsilon_{i}\}
,
V_{i}^{(2)}=\{(x^{*},y^{*})\in V_{i}\times V_{i}\mid\mathfrak{t}_{i}(x^{*})>\mathfrak{t}_{i}(y^{*})+\epsilon_{i}\}
and
\phi_{i}^{(2)}:=(\phi_{i},\phi_{i}):U_{i}^{(2)}\to V_{i}^{(2)}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\phi_{i}^{(2)})^{*}K^{i}-K_{Z}\|_{C^{[\epsilon_{i}^{-1}]}(U_{i}^{(2)})}\leq\epsilon_{i},

where
K^{i}
and
K_{Z}
denote the heat kernels on
(M_{i}\times\mathbb{I},g_{i}(t))
and
(\mathcal{R},g^{Z})
, respectively.

(d)

If
z_{i}^{*}\in M_{i}\times\mathbb{I}
converge to
z\in Z
in Gromov–Hausdorff sense, then

\displaystyle K^{i}(z_{i}^{*};\phi_{i}(\cdot))\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\quad\text{on}\quad\mathcal{R}_{(-\infty,t(z))}.

(e)

For each
t\in\mathbb{I}
, there are at most countable connected components of the time-slice
\mathcal{R}_{t}
.

For each
z\in Z
, we can assign a conjugate heat kernel measure
\nu_{z;s}:=K_{Z}(z;\cdot)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{Z}_{s}}
based at
z
for
s\leq\mathfrak{t}(z)
, which is a probability measure on
\mathcal{R}_{s}
. All these probability measures together satisfy the reproduction formula (cf.
[
FL25a
, Equation (5.5)]
).

Locally, one can define the tangent flow as follows.

Definition 2.20

(Tangent flow)
.

For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, a
tangent flow
at
z
is a pointed Gromov–Hausdorff limit of
(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))
for a sequence
r_{j}\searrow 0
.

It can be proved (cf.
[
FL25a
, Section 7]
) that any tangent flow is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space, and the regular part admits the structure of Ricci shrinker (cf.
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.9]
).

On
Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, we have the following regular-singular decomposition:

\displaystyle Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}\sqcup\mathcal{S},

where
\mathcal{R}_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
denotes the restriction of
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathbb{I}^{-}
. It can be proved (see
[
FL25a
, Theorem 7.15]
) that a point
z
is a regular point if and only if any of its tangent flow is isometric to
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0}^{n},0),\mathfrak{t})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{-},d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}},(\vec{0}^{n},0),\mathfrak{t})
, where
d^{*}_{E,\epsilon_{0}}
denotes the induced
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
using the spacetime distance constant
\epsilon_{0}
. Equivalently,
z
is a regular point if and only if
\mathcal{N}_{z}(0)\geq-\epsilon_{n}
(see
[
FL25a
, Proposition 7.7]
). It can be proved (see
[
FL25a
, Theorem 1.13]
) that the Minkowski dimension of
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
d_{Z}
is at most
n-2
.

Cylindrical and almost cylindrical points

Let
\mathcal{C}^{k}_{-1}
be as defined in Example
2.1
. We define
\mathcal{C}^{k}=(\bar{M},(\bar{g}(t))_{t<0},(\bar{f}(t))_{t<0})
to be the associated Ricci flow, where
t=0
corresponds to the singular time, and the potential function is given by

\displaystyle\bar{f}(t):=\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4|t|}+\frac{n-k}{4}+\Theta_{n-k},

where
\Theta_{n-k}
is the same constant defined in (
2.2
).

As shown in
[
FL25a
, Section 9]
, suppose that
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
is a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
, where
0
is the first singular time. We consider the
d^{*}
-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.99T,0)}
, defined in Definition
2.16
, using a fixed constant
\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(n,Y)>0
. We then define

\displaystyle(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})

to be the metric completion of
\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)}
with respect to
d^{*}
. By construction,
(Z_{[-0.98T,0)},d_{Z})=(\mathcal{X}_{[-0.98T,0)},d^{*})
; that is, the completion adds only the points in
Z_{0}
. Notice that
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
, which is called the
completion
of
\mathcal{X}
, is a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
[-0.98T,0]
.

Although the above construction is stated for closed Ricci flows, the same conclusion holds for any complete Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
, provided the curvature is bounded on every compact time interval within
[-T,0)
; see
[
FL25a
, Section 11]
.

In particular, we set the completion of
(\bar{M},(\bar{g}(t))_{t\in(-\infty,0)})
to be
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
, equipped with the spacetime distance
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
. It is straightforward to verify that the metric completion adds only the singular set
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\{0\}
to
\mathcal{C}^{k}
. We then define the base point
p^{*}
as the limit of
(\bar{p},t)
as
t\nearrow 0
with respect to
d_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}
, where
\bar{p}\in\bar{M}
is a minimum point of
\bar{f}(-1)
. It is clear that
p^{*}
is independent of the choice of
\bar{p}
. Moreover, for any
t<0
,

\displaystyle\nu_{p^{*};t}=(4\pi|t|)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-\bar{f}(t)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{g}(t)}.

In general, let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
, where
0
is the first singular time. Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is the completion of
\mathcal{X}
. Fix a point
z\in Z_{0}
. Then the potential function
f_{z}
is smooth for
t<0
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\nu_{z;t}=(4\pi|t|)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-f_{z}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}.

Definition 2.21

(Modified Ricci flow)
.

For any
z\in Z_{0}
, let
\phi_{t}
be the family of diffeomorphisms generated by
-\nabla_{g(t)}f_{z}(t)
with
\phi_{-T}=\mathrm{id}
. Then we define

\begin{cases}&g^{z}(s):=e^{s}\phi^{*}_{-e^{-s}}g(-e^{-s}),\\
&f^{z}(s):=\phi^{*}_{-e^{-s}}f_{z}(-e^{-s}).\\
\end{cases}

(M,g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
is called the
modified Ricci flow with respect to
z
. It is clear that
(g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\partial_{s}g^{z}(s)=g^{z}-2\mathrm{Ric}(g^{z})-2\nabla^{2}f^{z},\\
&\partial_{s}f^{z}(s)=\frac{n}{2}-\mathrm{R}_{g^{z}}-\Delta f^{z}.\end{cases}

(2.7)

Next, we consider a general noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
over
\mathbb{I}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,T,Y)
. Then, we have the following definition.

Definition 2.22
.

A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
a cylindrical point with respect to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
if a tangent flow at
z
(see Definition
2.20
) is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
for some
k
.

The following theorem regarding the uniqueness of cylindrical tangent flows was proved in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 6.2]
. Although
[
LZ23
, Theorem 6.2]
is stated for a singular point at the first singular time, the same proof works for any singular point in a Ricci flow limit space. We sketch the proof for readers’ convenience.

Theorem 2.23

(Uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow)
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
\mathbb{I}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,T,Y)
. For any
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
, if a tangent flow at
z
is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
, then any tangent flow at
z
is also
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

Proof.

We consider a metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{z}
associated with
z
. By our assumption, it implies that a tangent metric flow (see
[
FL25a
, Definition 4.13]
) at
z
is given by a cylinder
\mathcal{C}^{k}
. By the same proof of
[
LZ23
, Theorem 6.2]
, it implies that any tangent metric flow at
z
is
\mathcal{C}^{k}
. Now, we take a sequence
r_{j}\to 0
such that

\displaystyle(Z,r_{j}^{-1}d_{Z},z,r_{j}^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\xrightarrow[j\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})

in the Gromov–Hausdorff sense.

By
[
FL25a
, Theorem 4.17 (2)]
, and after passing to a further subsequence if necessary, the rescalings of
(\mathcal{X}^{z},\nu_{z;t})
by
r_{i}^{-1}
,
\mathbb{F}
-converge to a tangent metric flow at
z
, which must be given by
\mathcal{C}^{k}
as mentioned above. Thus, we conclude that
\mathcal{X}^{z^{\prime}}
, which is a metric flow associated with
z^{\prime}
, is given by
\mathcal{C}^{k}
.

From
[
FL25a
, Theorem 7.19]
, we know that

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-\infty,0)}=\mathcal{C}^{k}.

Consequently, it follows from
[
FL25a
, Theorem 7.25]
that
Z^{\prime}_{(0,+\infty)}=\emptyset
and hence
Z^{\prime}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.24
.

It is clear that Theorem
2.23
also holds in the case that one tangent flow is isometric to
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times N
equipped with the standard metric, where
N
is an
H
-stable Einstein manifold with the obstruction of order
3
.

Next, we recall the following definition from
[
FL25a
, Definition 5.37]
.

Definition 2.25

(
\epsilon
-close)
.

Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
and
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})
are two pointed noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces, with regular parts given by the Ricci flow spacetimes
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
and
(\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}},g^{Z^{\prime}})
, respectively, such that
J
is a time interval.

We say that
(Z,d_{Z},z,\mathfrak{t})
is
\epsilon
-close
to
(Z^{\prime},d_{Z^{\prime}},z^{\prime},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime})

over
J
if there exists an open set
U\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
and a smooth embedding
\phi:U\to\mathcal{R}_{J}
satisfying the following properties.

(a)

\phi
is time-preserving.

(b)

U\subset B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{J}
and
U
is an
\epsilon
-net of
B^{*}_{Z^{\prime}}(z^{\prime},\epsilon^{-1})\bigcap Z^{\prime}_{J}
with respect to
d_{Z^{\prime}}
.

(c)

For any
x,y\in U
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert d_{Z}(\phi(x),\phi(y))-d_{Z^{\prime}}(x,y)\right\rvert\leq\epsilon.

(d)

The
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\phi(U)
with respect to
d_{Z}
contains
B^{*}_{Z}(z,\epsilon^{-1}-\epsilon)\bigcap Z_{J}
.

(e)

There exists
x_{0}\in U
such that
d_{Z^{\prime}}(x_{0},z^{\prime})\leq\epsilon
and
d_{Z}(\phi(x_{0}),z)\leq\epsilon
.

(f)

On
U
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\rVert\phi^{*}g^{Z}-g^{Z^{\prime}}\rVert_{C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}(U)}+\rVert\phi^{*}\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}-\partial_{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}\rVert_{C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}(U)}\leq\epsilon.

Next, we define

Definition 2.26
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
\mathbb{I}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,T,Y)
. A point
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is called
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical
if
\mathfrak{t}(z)-\epsilon^{-1}r^{2}\in\mathbb{I}^{-}
and

\displaystyle(Z,r^{-1}d_{Z},z,r^{-2}(\mathfrak{t}-\mathfrak{t}(z)))\quad\text{is $\epsilon$-close to}\quad(\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k},d_{\mathcal{C}},p^{*},\mathfrak{t})\quad\text{over}\quad[-\epsilon^{-1},\epsilon^{-1}].

The following lemma follows immediately from smooth convergence and Definition
2.26
.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be a noncollapsed Ricci flow limit space over
\mathbb{I}
, obtained as the limit of a sequence in
\mathcal{M}(n,T,Y)
. For any
\delta>0
, if
\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\delta)
and
z\in Z_{\mathbb{I}^{-}}
is
(k,\epsilon,r)
-cylindrical, then there exists a diffeomorphism from
\{\bar{b}\leq\delta^{-1}\}\subset\bar{M}
, where
\bar{b}=2\sqrt{\bar{f}(-1)}
, onto a subset of
\mathcal{R}
such that

\displaystyle\|r^{-2}\varphi^{*}g^{Z}_{r^{2}t}-\bar{g}(t)\|_{C^{[\delta^{-1}]}}+\|\varphi^{*}f_{z}(r^{2}t)-\bar{f}(t)\|_{C^{[\delta^{-1}]}}\leq\delta,

(2.8)

on
\{\bar{b}\leq\delta^{-1}\}\times[-\delta^{-1},-\delta]
.

3
Remainder estimates for integral quantities

Throughout this section, let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow. We fix a spacetime point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and assume
\mathcal{X}
has entropy bounded below by
-Y
at
x_{0}^{*}
(see Definition
2.9
). Moreover, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)},

where
\tau=t_{0}-t
and
f=f_{x_{0}^{*}}
. For simplicity, we define

\begin{cases}&w:=\tau(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})+f-n,\\
&\mathcal{T}:=\tau\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}(\tau f)-\frac{g}{2}.\\
\end{cases}

Recall that Perelman’s differential Harnack inequality (see
[
Per02
, Section 9]
) states that
w\leq 0
. For the next proposition, we obtain the remainder estimates for the Nash entropy and
\mathcal{W}
-entropy.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose
[t,t_{0}]\subset I
and
\mathrm{R}\geq R_{\min}
on
M\times[t,t_{0}]
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\epsilon}=C(n,Y,R_{\min}(t_{0}-t),\epsilon)
such that

\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}\tau\left(|\nabla f|^{2}+|\mathrm{R}|+|\Delta f|\right)+\left\lvert f\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C_{\epsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{(4+\epsilon)(t_{0}-t)}\right)

for any
L>0
, where
(z,t)
is any
H_{n}
-center of
x_{0}^{*}
(see Definition
2.11
).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}-t=1
.

From
[
Bam20b
, Proposition 6.5]
, it follows that for any
\alpha\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\int_{M}e^{\alpha f}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq e^{(n-R_{\min})\alpha}.

By Theorem
2.13
(ii), it holds that
f\geq-C(n,Y)
, and thus, for any
p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C(n,Y,p)\int_{M}e^{f/2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C(n,Y,R_{\min},p).

(3.1)

By Hölder’s inequality, (
3.1
) and Theorem
2.13
(i), we obtain that for
p^{-1}+q^{-1}=1
,

\displaystyle\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}|f|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}|f|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y,p,R_{\min},\epsilon)\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{q(4+\epsilon)}\right).

If we choose
q
to be close to
1
and modify constants, we obtain

\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}\left\lvert f\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C_{\epsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{4+\epsilon}\right).

(3.2)

Next, we choose a cut-off function
\eta:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
such that
\eta(s)=0
if
s\leq L
and
\eta(s)=1
if
s\geq L+1
. Moreover,
|\eta^{\prime}|^{2}\leq 100\eta
. For
x\in M
, we also denote
\psi(x):=\eta(d_{t}(x,z))
.
Using integration by parts, we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{M}(|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^{2}+\mathrm{R})\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+2\int_{M}\langle\nabla f,\nabla\psi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}(n-f)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|\nabla f|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+2\int_{M}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{\psi}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}n+|f|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|\nabla f|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+200\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{4+\epsilon}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|\nabla f|^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t},

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
w\leq 0
, and in the last inequality we applied (
3.2
) and Theorem
2.13
(i). Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}|\nabla f|^{2}+|\mathrm{R}|\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C_{\epsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{4+\epsilon}\right).

(3.3)

By
w\leq 0
again and integration by parts, we have

\displaystyle 2\int_{M}|\Delta f|\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\left(|w|+|\nabla f|^{2}+|\mathrm{R}|+|f|+n\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\left(-w+|\nabla f|^{2}+|\mathrm{R}|+|f|+n\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\left(-2\Delta f+2|\nabla f|^{2}+2|\mathrm{R}|+2|f|+2n\right)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}

\displaystyle\leq 2\int_{M}(|\nabla f|^{2}+|\mathrm{R}|+|f|+n)\psi\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}+\int_{M\setminus B_{t}(z,L)}\frac{|\nabla\psi|^{2}}{\psi}\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}\leq C_{\epsilon}\exp\left(-\frac{L^{2}}{4+\epsilon}\right),

where we used (
3.2
), (
3.3
) and Theorem
2.13
(i).

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

4
Variations of the
\mathcal{W}
-functional near cylinders

In this section, we aim to derive the variations of the
\mathcal{W}
-functional (as in Definition
2.2
) of a weighted Riemannian manifold near a weighted cylinder.

Throughout this section, we fix
n\geq 3
,
m\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
, and consider the cylinder

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{n-m}\times S^{m},g_{E}\times g_{S^{m}},\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{m}{2}+\Theta_{m}\right),

(4.1)

as in Example
2.1
. In the following, the Sobolev spaces are defined with respect to the weighted measure
\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}
(cf. Definition
2.3
). The constant
C
appearing in this section depends only on
n
, and may vary from line to line.

Consider a compactly supported pair
(h,\chi)\in C^{2}(S^{2}(\bar{M}))\times C^{2}(\bar{M})
such that

(i)

(\bar{M},\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)
is a weighted Riemannian manifold;

(ii)

\displaystyle\|h\|_{C^{2}}+\|\chi\|_{C^{2}}:=\delta\ll 1
.

As in
[
CMI25
]
and
[
LZ23
]
, we have the following decomposition:

\displaystyle h=ug_{S^{m}}+\zeta,\quad\chi=\frac{m}{2}u+q,

(4.2)

where
ug_{S^{m}}
is the projection onto
\mathcal{K}_{0}g_{S^{m}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{0}
is the space of quadratic Hermite polynomials (see Definition
2.4
). For simplicity, we set

\displaystyle\alpha:=\|u\|_{L^{2}}.

For
u\in\mathcal{K}_{0}
, the following estimate holds (see
[
CMI25
, Lemma 6.4]
):

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists constant
C=C(n)
such that the following holds for
u\in\mathcal{K}_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-m}
:

|u|+|\vec{x}||\nabla u|+(1+|\vec{x}|^{2})|\nabla^{2}u|\leq C(1+|\vec{x}|^{2})\alpha.

We also need the following estimate from
[
LZ23
, Lemma 3.6]
:

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
k\geq 0
, there exists a constant
C_{k}=C_{k}(n)
such that for any tensor
T\in W^{k,2}(T^{r,s}\bar{M})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{M}}\bar{f}^{k}|T|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq C_{k}\rVert T\rVert_{W^{k,2}}^{2}.

Next, we recall that the center of mass vector
\mathcal{B}(h,\chi)=\left(\mathcal{B}_{1}(h,\chi),\cdots,\mathcal{B}_{n-m}(h,\chi)\right)
is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{i}(h,\chi):=\int_{\bar{M}}\bigg\langle\partial_{x_{i}},\bar{\nabla}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}_{\bar{g}}(h)-\chi\right)\bigg\rangle\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\bar{M}}x_{i}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}_{\bar{g}}(h)-\chi\right)\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}.

(4.3)

The following two rigidity inequalities are obtained in
[
CMI25
, Theorem 9.1]
(see also
[
LZ23
, Proposition 4.9, Theorem 4.10]
), provided that
\delta
is sufficiently small. Again, the norms of the Sobolev spaces are defined with respect to
\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exists a constant
C=C(n)
such that

\displaystyle\|\zeta\|_{W^{2,2}}+\|\bar{\nabla}q\|_{W^{1,2}}\leq C\left(\|\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}}h\|_{W^{1,2}}+|\mathcal{B}(h,\chi)|+\rVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\right).

Proposition 4.4
.

For any
\epsilon>0
,
there exist positive constants
C=C(n),C_{\epsilon}=C(n,\epsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}\leq C\|(1+|\vec{x}|)^{2}\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)\|_{L^{1}}+C_{\epsilon}\left(\|\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)\|^{2-\epsilon}_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}}h\|^{2-\epsilon}_{W^{1,2}}+|\mathcal{B}(h,\chi)|^{2-\epsilon}\right).

For simplicity, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}:=\|\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}}h\|_{W^{1,2}}+|\mathcal{B}(h,\chi)|.

Since
\|h\|_{C^{2}}+\|\chi\|_{C^{2}}=\delta
, we can easily get
\alpha+\mathfrak{X}\leq C\delta\ll 1
and
|\zeta|+|q|\leq C\delta(1+|\vec{x}|^{2})
. Moreover, by Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|(1+|\vec{x}|)^{2}\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)\|_{L^{1}}\leq C\|\mathbf{\Phi}(\bar{g}+h,\bar{f}+\chi)\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\mathfrak{X}.

Thus, it follows from Propositions
4.3
and
4.4
that

Corollary 4.5
.

We have

\displaystyle\|\zeta\|_{W^{2,2}}+\|\bar{\nabla}q\|_{W^{1,2}}\leq C\mathfrak{X}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha^{2}\leq C\mathfrak{X}.

Now, we consider the families for
s\in[0,1]
:

\displaystyle g(s)=\bar{g}+sh,\quad\text{and}\quad f(s)=\bar{f}+s\chi.

In addition, for the weighted Riemannian manifold
(\bar{M},g(s),f(s))
, we define

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\mathrm{d}V_{s}:=\mathrm{d}V_{f(s)},\\
&V(s)=\int_{\bar{M}}1\,\mathrm{d}V_{s},\\
&Q(s)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}_{g(s)}h-\chi,\\
&w(s):=2\Delta_{g(s)}f(s)-|\nabla_{g(s)}f(s)|^{2}+\mathrm{R}\left(g(s)\right)+f(s)-n,\\
&W(s)=\mathcal{W}(g(s),f(s))=\int_{\bar{M}}w(s)\,\mathrm{d}V_{s},\\
&\Phi(s)=\mathbf{\Phi}(g(s),f(s))=\frac{g(s)}{2}-\mathrm{Ric}(g(s))-\nabla^{2}_{g(s)}f(s).\end{cases}

For the rest of this section, all geometric quantities such as
\mathrm{Ric}
,
\nabla
,
\nabla^{2}
, and
\mathrm{div}_{f}
are defined with respect to the pair
(g(s),f(s))
, though we omit the subscript
g(s)
and the parameter
s
for simplicity. The norms
\|\cdot\|_{L^{1}}
,
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}}
,
|\cdot|
and
[\cdot]_{l}
(see (
2.3
)) are taken with respect to
\mathrm{d}V_{s}
and
g(s)
, respectively. Notice that since
\|h\|_{C^{2}}+\|\chi\|_{C^{2}}
is sufficiently small, the volume form
\mathrm{d}V_{s}
is uniformly comparable to
\mathrm{d}V_{0}=\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}
, and the norms
[\cdot]_{l}
are uniformly comparable to the corresponding norms defined with respect to
\bar{g}
for
l\leq 2
.

First, we recall the following result; see
[
CZ12
, Lemma 2.3]
for instance.

Lemma 4.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(0)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}h+\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}}^{*}\mathrm{div}_{\bar{f}}h+\nabla^{2}Q(0).

Next, we estimate:

Lemma 4.7
.

There exist a constant
C=C(n)
such that the following inequalities hold for any
s\in[0,1]
:

(i)

\displaystyle\left|\Phi^{\prime}(s)\right|\leq C\left([h]_{2}+[\nabla\chi]_{1}+(1+|\vec{x}|)[h]_{1}\right)
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\left|\Phi^{\prime\prime}(s)\right|\leq C\left([h]_{1}^{2}+[h]_{0}[h]_{2}+(1+|\vec{x}|)[h]_{0}[h]_{1}+[h]_{1}[\nabla\chi]_{0}\right)
.

Proof.

Recall the following local expression of
\mathrm{Ric},\nabla^{2}f
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}_{ij}=\partial_{l}\Gamma^{l}_{ij}-\partial_{i}\Gamma^{l}_{lj}+\Gamma^{a}_{ij}\Gamma^{l}_{la}-\Gamma^{a}_{lj}\Gamma^{l}_{ia},\quad\nabla_{ij}^{2}f=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}f-\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\partial_{k}f.

Set
C^{k}_{ij}:=\left(\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\right)_{s}
and
D^{k}_{ij}:=\left(\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\right)_{ss}
. Since
\Gamma^{k}_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}g^{kl}\left(\partial_{i}g_{jl}+\partial_{j}g_{il}-\partial_{l}g_{ij}\right)
, we have for
s\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle C^{k}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}g^{kl}\left(\partial_{i}(g_{s})_{jl}+\partial_{j}(g_{s})_{il}-\partial_{l}(g_{s})_{ij}\right)+\frac{1}{2}(g^{kl})_{s}\left(\partial_{i}g_{jl}+\partial_{j}g_{il}-\partial_{l}g_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}g^{kl}\left(\partial_{i}h_{jl}+\partial_{j}h_{il}-\partial_{l}h_{ij}\right)-\frac{1}{2}h^{kl}\left(\partial_{i}g_{jl}+\partial_{j}g_{il}-\partial_{l}g_{ij}\right)

(4.4)

and

\displaystyle D^{k}_{ij}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(g^{kl})_{s}\left(\partial_{i}h_{jl}+\partial_{j}h_{il}-\partial_{l}h_{ij}\right)-\frac{1}{2}(h^{kl})_{s}\left(\partial_{i}g_{jl}+\partial_{j}g_{il}-\partial_{l}g_{ij}\right)
