x^{*}\in\Omega_{i}:=\varphi_{A,i}\left(\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}\}\right)\times[-2,-1/2]
, where
r^{i}_{\mathrm{Rm}}
denotes the curvature radius of
\mathcal{X}^{i}
(see Definition
2.10
). Therefore, by the definition of
\textbf{r}_{A}
, the convergence (
6.35
) is smooth on
\Omega_{i}
, and the domains
(\Omega_{i},g_{i}(t),q_{i}^{*})
, via the diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}
, converge smoothly to a domain
\Omega\subset Z
containing the point
q
. Moreover, it follows from our construction that the maps
\varphi_{A,i}^{-1}\circ\phi_{i}:\Omega\to\bar{M}
converge smoothly to an isometry:

\displaystyle\psi:\Omega\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m})\times[-2,-1/2],

(6.36)

where the half-cylinder
\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m}
is equipped with a family of standard metrics
g_{c}(t)
for
t\in[-2,-1/2]
. For simplicity, we set
q(t)\in\Omega_{t}
to be the flow line of
\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}
from
q
.

Next, we consider a metric flow
\mathcal{X}^{q}
associated with
q
(cf. Theorem
2.18
) and set
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\iota_{q}(\mathcal{R}^{q})\subset\mathcal{R}
, where
\mathcal{R}^{q}
denotes the regular part of
\mathcal{X}^{q}
. It is clear from our construction that
\Omega_{[-2,-1/2)}\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

We consider an auxiliary function:

\displaystyle F_{i}^{\prime}(x,t):=2\frac{\tilde{F}_{i}(x,t)-\tilde{F}_{i}(q_{i},-1)}{L_{i}}.

(6.37)

By a direct computation (see (
6.17
)), we have

\displaystyle\square F_{i}^{\prime}=-\frac{n}{L_{i}}.

(6.38)

We aim to show that the limit of
F_{i}^{\prime}
exists on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
, where
\epsilon:=10^{-5}\sigma
.

First, it follows from Proposition
6.7
and our assumption on
L_{i}
that

\int_{-2}^{-1/2}\int_{M_{i}}\left|\tau\mathrm{Ric}(g_{i})+\nabla_{i}^{2}\tilde{F}_{i}-\frac{g_{i}}{2}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0,i}^{*};t}\mathrm{d}t\leq Ce^{-\frac{L_{i}^{2}}{4(1-\sigma)^{2}}}.

(6.39)

Claim 6.15
.

For any compact subset
S
of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
, we have smooth convergence of
\phi_{i}^{*}\big(\nabla_{i}^{2}\tilde{F}_{i}\big)
as symmetric
2
-tensors.

For any
z\in S
, there exists a smooth spacetime curve
\gamma(s)\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{s}
for
s\in[\mathfrak{t}(z),-1+\epsilon]
, such that
\gamma(\mathfrak{t}(z))=z
and
\gamma(-1+\epsilon)=q(-1+\epsilon)
. Since
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon]}
is connected, such a curve always exists. Moreover, because
S
is compact, one can choose
\gamma
so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)}^{-1+\epsilon}|\mathrm{R}_{g^{Z}}(\gamma(s))|+|\gamma^{\prime}(s)|^{2}_{g^{Z}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C(S),

where
C(S)
depends only on the compact
S
and the geometry of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, but is independent of the choice of
z
in
S
. Since
\phi_{i}^{-1}((q_{i},-1+\epsilon))\to q(-1+\epsilon)
and
r_{\mathrm{Rm}}^{i}((q_{i},-1+\epsilon))\geq c_{0}
, the curve
\gamma
can be used to construct a corresponding spacetime curve
\gamma_{i}(s)\in\mathcal{X}^{i}_{s}
for
s\in[\mathfrak{t}(z),-1+\epsilon]
so that
\gamma_{i}(\mathfrak{t}(z))=\phi_{i}(z)
,
\gamma_{i}(-1+\epsilon)=(q_{i},-1+\epsilon)
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)}^{-1+\epsilon}|\mathrm{R}_{i}(\gamma_{i}(s))|+|\gamma_{i}^{\prime}(s)|^{2}_{g_{i}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C(S),

which is independent of
i
. In fact,
\gamma_{i}
can be obtained by concatenating
\phi_{i}(\gamma)
with a short curve in a neighborhood of
(q_{i},-1+\epsilon)
.

By Perelman’s differential Harnack inequality (see
[
Per02
, Corollary 9.4]
), we obtain that if
(x,\mathfrak{t}(z))=\phi_{i}(z)
, then

\displaystyle\sqrt{|\mathfrak{t}(z)|}f_{i}(x,\mathfrak{t}(z)))\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\epsilon}f_{i}(q_{i},-1+\epsilon)+\int_{\mathfrak{t}(z)}^{-1+\epsilon}\sqrt{|s|}\left(|\mathrm{R}_{i}(\gamma_{i}(s))|+|\gamma_{i}^{\prime}(s)|_{g_{i}(t)}^{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\epsilon}f_{i}(q_{i},-1+\epsilon)+C(S).

Thus, for any
(x,t)\in\phi_{i}(S)
, we have

\displaystyle f_{i}(x,t)\leq f_{i}(q_{i},-1+\epsilon)+C(S)\leq\frac{(1+2\epsilon)L_{i}^{2}}{4}+C(S),

where we used Proposition
6.3
for the last inequality. It implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{x_{0,i}^{*};t}(x)=(4\pi\tau)^{-n/2}e^{-f_{i}(x,t)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g_{i}(t)}\geq c(n)\exp\left(-\frac{(1+2\epsilon)L_{i}^{2}}{4}-C(S)\right)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g_{i}(t)}.

Combining this with (
6.39
), we obtain

\displaystyle\iint_{\phi_{i}(S)}\left|\tau\mathrm{Ric}(g_{i})+\nabla_{i}^{2}\tilde{F}_{i}-\frac{g_{i}}{2}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g_{i}(t)}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\exp\left(-\frac{L_{i}^{2}}{4(1-\sigma)^{2}}+\frac{(1+2\epsilon)L_{i}^{2}}{4}+C(S)\right)\leq C(S)e^{-\sigma L_{i}^{2}/4},

(6.40)

where the last inequality holds since
\epsilon=10^{-5}\sigma
.

We set
\mathcal{T}_{i}:=\tau\mathrm{Ric}_{i}+\nabla_{i}^{2}\tilde{F}_{i}-g_{i}/2
. Then we have
(\partial_{t}-\Delta_{i,L})\mathcal{T}_{i}=0
, where
\Delta_{i,L}
denotes the Lichnerowicz Laplacian with respect to
g_{i}(t)
. Therefore, it follows from the standard parabolic regularity theory and the unweighted
L^{2}
-bound in (
6
) that
\phi_{i}^{*}(\mathcal{T}_{i})
converge smoothly to
0
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
. In particular,
\phi_{i}^{*}(\nabla_{i}^{2}\tilde{F}_{i})
converge smoothly to a smooth symmetric
2
-tensor on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
. This completes the proof of the Claim
6.15
.

Since
\nabla^{2}_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}=2\nabla_{i}^{2}\tilde{F}_{i}/L_{i}
and
L_{i}\to\infty
, it follows from Claim
6.15
and (
6.38
) that

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}\to 0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\square_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}\to 0\quad\text{smoothly on}\quad\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}.

(6.41)

Claim 6.16
.

The following equations hold:

\displaystyle F_{i}^{\prime}(q_{i},-1)=0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\left|\nabla_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}(q_{i},-1)\right|=1+\Psi(L_{i}^{-1}).

The first equality follows from the definition. For the second, first note that for the standard potential function
\bar{F}
on the cylinder, we have
2\left|\nabla\bar{F}(q_{i})\right|/L_{i}=1+\Psi(L_{i}^{-1})
. By Definition
5.1
(A), we have

\displaystyle\left|\,|\nabla\bar{F}(q_{i})|-|\nabla_{i}F_{i}(q_{i},-1)|\,\right|\leq\Psi(L_{i}^{-1}).

Thus, we also have

\displaystyle 2\frac{\left|\nabla_{i}F_{i}(q_{i},-1)\right|}{L_{i}}=1+\Psi(L_{i}^{-1}).

(6.42)

By Lemma
6.10
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{i}(F_{i}-\tilde{F}_{i})(q_{i},-1)\right\rvert\leq\Psi(L_{i}^{-1}).

(6.43)

Combining (
6.42
) and (
6.43
), we conclude that

\displaystyle 2\frac{\left|\nabla_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}(q_{i},-1)\right|}{L_{i}}=1+\Psi(L_{i}^{-1}),

which gives the second inequality. This finishes the proof of the Claim
6.16
.

By (
6.41
) and Claim
6.16
, we conclude that
\phi_{i}^{*}(F_{i}^{\prime})
converge smoothly to a smooth function
F_{\infty}
on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
. It follows from (
6.41
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathfrak{t}}F_{\infty}=\nabla^{2}F_{\infty}=0

on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
. Moreover, we have by Claim
6.16

\displaystyle F_{\infty}(q(-1))=0\quad\text{and}\quad|\nabla F_{\infty}|(q(-1))=1.

Thus, it follows from
[
FL25a
, Theorem 4.19]
(see also
[
Bam20b
, Theorem 15.50]
) that
\nabla F_{\infty}
induces a splitting direction on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
. On the other hand, by our construction of
F_{\infty}
, we conclude that
\psi_{*}(\nabla F_{\infty})
agrees with the splitting direction of
\mathbb{R}_{-}
in
(\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m})\times(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)
; see (
6.36
). Thus, there exists a domain
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
containing
\Omega_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}
so that
\psi
can be extended to an isometry:

\displaystyle\psi:\Omega^{\prime}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m})\times(-3/2,-1+\epsilon).

Since
\Omega^{\prime}_{t}
is complete for any
t\in(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}=\iota_{q}(\mathcal{X}^{q}_{(-3/2,-1+\epsilon)}).

From the definition (
6.37
) of
F_{i}^{\prime}
, we conclude that before we reach the limit,

\displaystyle V_{i}:=\nabla_{i}F_{i}^{\prime}=2\frac{\nabla_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}}{L_{i}}

is almost a splitting vector field. It is important to note that although the definition of
F_{i}^{\prime}
depends on the choice of base point
q_{i}
, the definition of
V_{i}
is independent of
q_{i}
.

Since the above argument holds uniformly for any choice of
q_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
, we conclude that on
B_{g_{i}(-1)}(\Sigma_{i},100D_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert|V_{i}|-1\right\rvert\leq\Psi(i^{-1})\quad\text{and}\quad[\nabla_{i}V_{i}]_{100}\leq\Psi(i^{-1}).

(6.44)

On the other hand, we set
\bar{V}
to be the vector field
\partial_{r}
on
\bar{M}
, where
r=|\vec{x}|
. We set the flow lines of
V_{i}
and
\bar{V}
by
\psi^{i}_{s}
and
\bar{\psi}_{s}
, respectively, such that
\psi^{i}_{0}=\bar{\psi}_{0}=\mathrm{id}
. Using the flow lines, we obtain a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\kappa_{i}:\Sigma_{i}\times[0,50D_{1}]\to M_{i}

defined by
\kappa_{i}(w,s)=\psi^{i}_{s}(w)
. It is clear from (
6.44
) that if
i
is sufficiently large,
\kappa_{i}
is
C^{10}
-close to an isometry up to an error
\Psi(i^{-1})
. Similarly, we define

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{i}:\{\bar{b}=L_{i}\}\times[0,50D_{1}]\to\bar{M}

defined by
\bar{\kappa}_{i}(w,s)=\bar{\psi}_{s}(w)
. Now, we can define
\varphi_{i}^{\prime}:\{L_{i}\leq\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+40D_{1}\}\to M_{i}
by

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}^{\prime}:=\kappa_{i}\circ\hat{\iota}\circ(\bar{\kappa}_{i})^{-1},

(6.45)

where
\hat{\iota}(w,s):=(\varphi_{A,i}(w),s)
for any
w\in\{\bar{b}=L_{i}\}
is
C^{5}
-close to an isometry up to an error of
\Psi(i^{-1})
. From our construction, it is clear that
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}
is
C^{5}
-close to an isometry up to an error
\Psi(i^{-1})
.

Choose a cut-off function
\eta:\bar{M}\to[0,1]
such that
\eta=1
on
\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+1/2\}
and
\mathrm{supp}(\eta)\subset\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+1\}
, then we can glue
\varphi_{i}^{\prime}
with
\varphi_{A,i}
by defining

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(x)=\eta(x)\varphi_{A,i}(x)+\left(1-\eta(x)\right)\varphi_{i}^{\prime}(x),\quad\forall x\in\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+20D_{1}\}.

(6.46)

Since
\varphi_{A,i}
is
C^{5}
-close to an isometry on
\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+10\}
, we can assume that
\varphi_{A,i}(x)
and
\varphi_{i}^{\prime}(x)
lie in a chart of a fixed coordinate system for
x\in\{L_{i}+1/2\leq\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+1\}
. Therefore, (
6.46
) is well-defined.
From the construction, we see that on
\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+20D_{1}\}
,

\displaystyle\left[(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime})^{*}g_{i}(-1)-\bar{g}(-1)\right]_{2}\leq\Psi(i^{-1}).

Since
L_{i}+20D_{1}>\textbf{r}_{A}^{i}+D_{0}
, we obtain a contradiction for sufficiently large
i
.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

Next, for fixed
\sigma\in(0,1/10)
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{l}=\bar{D}_{l}(n,\sigma)=10^{4}\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}\gg 1.

(6.47)

By Theorem
6.14
, we fix a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}_{A}
from
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}+\bar{D}_{l}\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}g(-1)-\bar{g}\right]_{2}\leq\bar{\delta}_{l}

(6.48)

on
\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}_{A}+\bar{D}_{l}\}
, and

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}g(-1)\right]_{5}+\left[\bar{f}-\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}f(-1)\right]_{5}\leq e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{\textbf{r}_{A}^{2}}{16}}

(6.49)

on
\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}_{A}-2\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}\}
.

Corollary 6.17
.

There exists a large constant
L^{\prime\prime}=L^{\prime\prime}(n,Y,\sigma,l)>1
such that if
\mathbf{r}_{A}\in[L^{\prime\prime},(1-2\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}]
, then on
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D}_{l}\}\times\{-1\}
,

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}\left(\frac{g}{2}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right)\right]_{l}\leq\exp\left(\frac{\bar{f}}{2}-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D}_{l})^{2}}{8(1-\sigma)}\right),

where the norm is with respect to
\bar{g}
. Moreover, on
\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\left(\left\{2\sqrt{\bar{f}(-1)}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}-2.5\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}\right\}\right)
,

\displaystyle\left[\frac{g}{2}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right]_{l}\leq\exp\left(\frac{f}{2}-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.4\bar{D}_{l})^{2}}{8(1-\sigma)}\right),

(6.50)

where the norm is with respect to
g(-1)
.

Proof.

It is straightforward from our parameter choices that
\textbf{r}_{A}+\bar{D}_{l}-\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}\leq(1-\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}
, provided that
L^{\prime\prime}
is sufficiently large. Therefore, the first conclusion follows from Proposition
6.13
by setting
\bar{L}=\textbf{r}_{A}+\bar{D}_{l}-\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}
. The second conclusion then follows from the first together with Proposition
6.2
and (
6.49
).
∎

Comparison of radius functions III

Next, we prove the following crucial extension result.

Theorem 6.18
.

There exists a large constant
\hat{L}=\hat{L}(n,Y,\sigma)>1
such that if
\mathbf{r}_{A}\in[\hat{L},(1-2\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}]
, then

\displaystyle\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\geq\mathbf{r}_{A}+\bar{D}_{100}/10.

Here, both
\textbf{r}_{A}
and
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
denote the radius functions for
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
(cf. Definition
5.1
), and
\bar{D}_{100}
is the constant defined in (
6.47
) with
l=100
.

The proof of this theorem follows the same overall strategy as in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
, but with two key differences.

First, while the weighted Riemannian manifold considered in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
is an exact Ricci shrinker, in our case
(M,g(-1),f(-1))
is only an almost Ricci shrinker, as ensured by Corollary
6.17
.

Second, in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
, the authors used the pseudolocality theorem and the self-similarity of Ricci shrinkers to produce a neck region with bounded geometry. That approach does not directly apply in our setting. However, Theorem
6.14
allows us to obtain a similar neck region, albeit of smaller size compared to that in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
.
Nevertheless, this smaller neck region suffices for our proof.

We now sketch the main ideas of the argument. As in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
, one can glue the end of
\bar{M}
to
M
through the neck region, resulting in a weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(\bar{M},g^{\prime},f^{\prime}\right)
that is almost a Ricci shrinker. Following the strategy of
[
CMI25
]
, we then use the first
n-m
eigenfunctions of the weighted
\Delta_{f^{\prime}}
along with their growth estimates (see
[
LZ23
, Proposition A.1]
and
[
CMI25
, Theorem 4.1]
) to extend the almost
(n-m)
-splitting to a larger region. Finally, we construct a diffeomorphism from the cylinder to this extended region such that the estimates in the definition of
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}
are satisfied.

Proof.

We choose
\hat{L}\geq\max\{L^{\prime}(n,Y,\sigma,100,\bar{D}_{100}),L^{\prime\prime}(n,Y,\sigma,100)\}
, where
L^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime\prime}
are from Theorem
6.14
and Corollary
6.17
, respectively. In the proof, the constant
C
may be different from line to line, depending on
n
,
Y
, and
\sigma
. We also use the notations
C_{k}
to represent constants that depend on
n
,
Y
,
\sigma
and
k
.

We set
(g,f)=(g(-1),f(-1))
,
L=\mathbf{r}_{A}-3\bar{\delta}_{100}^{-1}
, and
\bar{D}=\bar{D}_{100}
for simplicity. Moreover, we consider the diffeomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}_{A}
satisfying (
6.48
) and (
6.49
). In particular, we have

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}g-\bar{g}\right]_{5}+\left[\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}f-\bar{f}\right]_{5}\leq e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{L^{2}}{16}},\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\{\bar{b}\leq L\}.

(6.51)

It follows from Proposition
6.3
and Corollary
6.6
that

\displaystyle(1-\bar{\epsilon})\bar{F}-C\leq\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}f\leq(1+\bar{\epsilon})\bar{F}+C.

(6.52)

and

\displaystyle[\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}f]_{100}\leq Ce^{\bar{\epsilon}\bar{F}},

(6.53)

on
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D}\}
, where
\bar{\epsilon}=10^{-50}n^{-1}\sigma
.

Let
\phi^{t},\bar{\phi}^{t}
be the family of diffeomorphisms generated by
V:=\frac{\nabla b}{|\nabla b|^{2}},\bar{V}:=\frac{\bar{\nabla}\bar{b}}{|\bar{\nabla}\bar{b}|^{2}}
, respectively, with
\phi^{0}=\bar{\phi}^{0}=\mathrm{id}
. Define a map
\psi_{1}:\{L-2\leq\bar{b}\leq L-1\}\subset\bar{M}\to\left\{L-2\leq b\leq L-1\right\}
by:

\psi_{1}(x):=\phi^{\bar{b}(x)-b(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(x))}(\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(x))

Note that
\bar{b}=\psi_{1}^{*}b
. Moreover, it follows from (
6.51
) that
\bar{b}
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}b
are
C^{5}
-close. Moreover,
V
and
\bar{V}
are
C^{4}
-close under
\mathrm{d}\varphi_{A}
on
\{\bar{b}\leq L-1\}
. Therefore,
\psi_{1}
is a diffeomorphism such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\psi_{1}^{*}g\right]_{4}\leq Ce^{-CL}.

Next, we fix a cut-off function
\eta
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\eta=1
on
(-\infty,L-2]
and
\eta=0
on
[L-3/2,\infty)
. Then we define
\psi_{2}:\{\bar{b}\leq L-1\}\to\left\{b\leq L-1\right\}
by

\psi_{2}(x):=\eta\left(\bar{b}(x)\right)\tilde{\varphi}_{A}(x)+\left(1-\eta(\bar{b}(x))\right)\psi_{1}(x).

Since
\psi_{1}
is close to
\tilde{\varphi}_{A}
in the
C^{5}
sense on
\{L-2\leq b\leq L-1\}
, we can always assume that
\psi_{1}(x)
and
x
lie in a chart of a fixed coordinate system so that the above definition is well-defined. Moreover, we conclude that
\psi_{2}
is a diffeomorphism such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\psi_{2}^{*}g\right]_{4}+\left[\bar{f}-\psi_{2}^{*}f\right]_{4}\leq e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}

on
\{b\leq L-2\}
and

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\psi_{2}^{*}g\right]_{4}+\left[\bar{f}-\psi_{2}^{*}f\right]_{4}\leq Ce^{-CL}

on
\{L-2\leq b\leq L-1\}
.

By Corollary
6.17
, we see that on
\{\bar{b}\leq L+0.5\bar{D}\}
,

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{\varphi}_{A}^{*}\left(\frac{g}{2}-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}\right)\right]_{100}\leq\exp\left(\frac{\bar{f}}{2}-\frac{(L+0.5\bar{D})^{2}}{8(1-\sigma)}\right)\leq e^{-\frac{\sigma L^{2}}{10}}.

(6.54)

Set
\Omega:=\tilde{\varphi}_{A}\left(\{\bar{b}\leq L+0.5\bar{D}\}\right)
and
\Phi:=g/2-\nabla^{2}f-\mathrm{Ric}
. By the weighted Bianchi identity (see Lemma
2.5
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla\left(f-\mathrm{R}-|\nabla f|^{2}\right)=\nabla\left(\mathrm{Tr}(\Phi)\right)-\mathrm{div}_{f}(\Phi).

From (
6.53
), (
6.54
), and the fact that
\bar{\epsilon}\ll\sigma
, we obtain on
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla\left(f-\mathrm{R}-|\nabla f|^{2}\right)\right\rvert\leq e^{-\frac{\sigma L^{2}}{20}}.

Thus, it follows from (
6.51
) that there exists a constant
a_{0}
with
|a_{0}|\leq C(n)
such that on
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert f-\mathrm{R}-|\nabla f|^{2}-a_{0}\right\rvert\leq e^{-\frac{\sigma L^{2}}{30}}.

(6.55)

From this, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{|\nabla b|^{2}}-1\right|=\left|\frac{f}{|\nabla f|^{2}}-1\right|=\left|\frac{f}{f-\mathrm{R}+a_{0}+\Psi(L^{-1})}-1\right|\leq C(1+|f|)^{-1},

(6.56)

where we used (
6.52
) for the last inequality.

Now, we extend
\psi_{2}
to a diffeomorphism
\psi_{3}
from
\{\bar{b}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\}
onto a subset of
\Omega
by

\psi_{3}(x):=\phi^{\bar{b}(x)-L+1}\circ\psi_{1}\circ\bar{\phi}^{L-1-\bar{b}(x)}(x)

(6.57)

for any
x\in\{L-1\leq\bar{b}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\}
. Notice that by (
6.48
) and (
6.56
),
\psi_{3}
is well-defined. Moreover, by our construction, we know that
\bar{b}=\psi_{3}^{*}b
on
\{L-1\leq\bar{b}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\}
.

Claim 6.19
.

The above
\psi_{3}
satisfies:

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\psi_{3}^{*}g\right]_{4}+\left[\bar{f}-\psi_{3}^{*}f\right]_{4}\leq Ce^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{L^{2}}{16}},\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\{\bar{b}\leq L-2\};

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\psi_{3}^{*}g\right]_{4}+\left[\bar{f}-\psi_{3}^{*}f\right]_{4}\leq Ce^{-CL}\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\{L-2\leq\bar{b}\leq L-1\};

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\psi_{3}^{*}g\right]_{2}\leq CL^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\bar{f}=\psi_{3}^{*}f\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\{L-1\leq\bar{b}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\}.

The first two estimates follow from the construction directly, so we focus on the third estimate.

Set
\Sigma=\{b=L-1\}\cap\Omega
and let
g_{\Sigma}
denote the induced metric of
g
on
\Sigma
. Define
\psi_{4}:\Sigma\times[L-1,L+0.4\bar{D}]\to\{L-1\leq b\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\}\cap\Omega
by

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(w,r)=\phi^{r-L+1}(w)

for any
w\in\Sigma
. Then we claim that on
\Sigma\times[L-1,L+0.4\bar{D}]
,

\displaystyle\rVert\psi_{4}^{*}g-(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g_{\Sigma})\rVert_{C^{2}}\leq CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.58)

In fact, it follows from (
6.56
) that

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{4}^{*}g(\partial_{r},\partial_{r})-1\right|\leq CL^{-2}.

This implies that
\left|\psi_{4}^{*}g-(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g_{r})\right|\leq CL^{-2}
,
where we set
g_{r}
to be the induced metric on
\Sigma\times\{r\}
of
\psi_{4}^{*}g
. On the other hand, we compute

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}_{V}g\right|=\left|\mathcal{L}_{\frac{\sqrt{f}}{|\nabla f|^{2}}\nabla f}g\right|\leq\frac{\sqrt{f}}{|\nabla f|^{2}}\left|\mathcal{L}_{\nabla f}g\right|+C|\nabla f|\left|\nabla\frac{\sqrt{f}}{|\nabla f|^{2}}\right|.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\nabla f}g=2\nabla^{2}f
, which is bounded by
C(n)
from (
6.54
), it follows from (
6.55
) that on
\Omega
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}_{V}g\right|\leq CL^{-1}.

Thus, we obtain
|\partial_{r}g_{r}|\leq CL^{-1}
, which implies

\displaystyle|g_{r}-g_{\Sigma}|\leq CL^{-1}.

Consequently, we have on
\Sigma\times[L-1,L+0.45\bar{D}]
,

\displaystyle\rVert\psi_{4}^{*}g-(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+g_{\Sigma})\rVert_{C^{0}}\leq CL^{-1},

which implies the
C^{0}
-estimate in (
6.58
). The higher-order estimates follow from the standard interpolation, since by the two-sided pseudolocality theorem (see Theorem
2.14
) and Shi’s local estimates, we have the higher-order curvature estimates for both
g
and
g_{\Sigma}
.

Since
\|\psi_{3}^{*}g-\bar{g}\|_{C^{2}}\leq Ce^{-CL}
on
\{\bar{b}\leq L-1\}
, it follows from the definition of
\psi_{3}
(see (
6.57
)) and (
6.58
) that

\displaystyle\|\psi_{3}^{*}g-\bar{g}\|_{C^{2}}\leq Ce^{-CL}+CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}\leq CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}

on
\{L-1\leq\bar{b}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\}
. This completes the proof of Claim
6.19
.

Choose a cut-off function
\eta_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\eta_{1}=1
on
(-\infty,L+0.3\bar{D}]
and
\eta_{1}=0
on
[L+0.4\bar{D},\infty)
. Then we construct a complete weighted Riemannian manifold
(\bar{M},g^{\prime},f^{\prime})
by:

g^{\prime}:=\eta_{1}(\bar{b})\psi_{3}^{*}g+(1-\eta_{1}(\bar{b}))\bar{g},\quad f^{\prime}:=\eta_{1}(\bar{b})\psi_{3}^{*}f+\left(1-\eta_{1}(\bar{b})\right)\bar{f}.

For the rest of the proof, we set
b^{\prime}:=2\sqrt{|f^{\prime}|}
, and the underlying metric is
g^{\prime}
by default.

Claim 6.20
.

Define two functions
R_{1}:=n/2-\Delta f^{\prime}
and
R_{2}:=f^{\prime}-|\nabla f^{\prime}|^{2}
. On
(\bar{M},g^{\prime},f^{\prime})
, the following estimates hold:

|R_{1}|+|R_{2}|\leq CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b^{\prime})^{2}+C.

Note that on the standard cylinder, we have
n/2-\bar{\Delta}\bar{f}=\bar{f}-|\bar{\nabla}\bar{f}|^{2}=m/2
. Thus, the conclusion holds for
\{b^{\prime}\geq L+0.4\bar{D}\}
.

On
\{b^{\prime}<L+0.4\bar{D}\}
, it follows from (
4.9
), Claim
6.19
and our construction that

|\nabla f^{\prime}|\leq Cb^{\prime}+C\quad\text{and}\quad|\nabla^{2}f^{\prime}|\leq CL^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Thus, by Claim
6.19
and a standard variational argument, we obtain

\left\lvert R_{1}-m/2\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-CL}b^{\prime}+CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}b^{\prime}+C\leq CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}b^{\prime}+C

and

\left\lvert R_{2}-m/2\right\rvert\leq Ce^{-CL}(b^{\prime})^{2}+CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b^{\prime})^{2}+C\leq CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b^{\prime})^{2}+C.

This completes the proof of Claim
6.20
.

Claim
6.20
allows us to apply
[
LZ23
, Proposition A.1]
to get slow exponential growth of eigentensors. Let
0<\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\cdots
be the eigenvalue of
\Delta_{f^{\prime}}
with respect to
\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}
counted with multiplicities. And we choose
(n-m)
-orthonormal eigenfunctions
v_{i}
with
\Delta_{f^{\prime}}v_{i}+\mu_{i}v_{i}=0
for
1\leq i\leq n-m
with

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{M}}v_{i}v_{j}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}=\delta_{ij},\quad\int_{\bar{M}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}=\mu_{i}.

(6.59)

Claim 6.21
.

For
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-m\}
,

\rVert\nabla^{2}v_{i}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left|\mu_{i}-1/2\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}.

(6.60)

(
6.60
) can be proved in a similar way as
[
CMI25
, Lemma 7.4]
and
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
. Choose
\bar{c}
such that
\int_{M}\bar{c}^{2}dV_{\bar{f}}=1
. Let
\bar{\omega}_{0}=\bar{c}
,
\bar{\omega}_{i}=\frac{\bar{c}}{\sqrt{2}}x_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n-m
. By direct computation, we have

\displaystyle\left|\delta_{ij}-\int_{\bar{b}<L}\bar{\omega}_{i}\bar{\omega}_{j}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq CL^{n-m}e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4}},\quad\forall i,j\in\{0,1,\ldots,n-m\},

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}-\int_{\bar{b}<L}\langle\nabla\bar{\omega}_{i},\nabla\bar{\omega}_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq CL^{n-m}e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4}},\quad\forall i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n-m\}.

(6.61)

Choose a cut-off function
\eta_{2}
which equals to 1 on
\left\{b^{\prime}<L-1\right\}
and equals to 0 outside
\left\{b^{\prime}<L\right\}
. Set
\omega_{i}=\eta_{2}\bar{\omega}_{i}
and define
a_{ij}
and
b_{ij}
by

a_{ij}:=\int_{\bar{M}}\omega_{i}\omega_{j}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}},\quad b_{ij}:=\int_{\bar{M}}\langle\nabla\omega_{i},\nabla\omega_{j}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}.

By (
6.51
) and (
6
) (see also
[
CMI25
, Section 7]
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{i,j\geq 0}\left|a_{ij}-\delta_{ij}\right|+\sup_{i,j\geq 1}\left|2b_{ij}-\delta_{ij}\right|+\left|b_{00}\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}.

From the min-max principle, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n-m}\mu_{i}\leq\sum_{i,j}a^{ij}b_{ij}\leq\frac{n-m}{2}+Ce^{-\frac{L^{2}}{16}},

(6.62)

where
(a^{ij})
is the inverse matrix of
(a_{ij})
.

Set
h_{i}=\mu_{i}g^{\prime}-\mathrm{Ric}(g^{\prime})-\nabla^{2}f^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}:=\mathbf{\Phi}(g^{\prime},f^{\prime})=g^{\prime}/2-\mathrm{Ric}(g^{\prime})-\nabla^{2}f^{\prime}
. By our construction,
h_{i}=(\mu_{i}-1/2)g^{\prime}
on
S_{1}:=\left\{b^{\prime}>L+0.4\bar{D}\right\}
, and
|h_{i}|\leq CL^{\frac{1}{2}}
on
S_{2}:=\left\{L-2\leq b^{\prime}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}\right\}
. On
S_{3}:=\left\{b^{\prime}<L-2\right\}
, it holds that
h_{i}=\Phi^{\prime}+(\mu_{i}-1/2)g^{\prime}
, and by Corollary
6.17
we see that

\displaystyle\int_{S_{3}}\left|\Phi^{\prime}\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}\leq Ce^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4}}.

(6.63)

Recall the following Bochner formula:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{f^{\prime}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}=|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}-h_{i}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i}).

(6.64)

Since
|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}
is integrable by (
6.59
), we can obtain from (
6.64
) that
|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}
is integrable. In fact, for
A>100
, we choose a cutoff function
\eta_{A}:\bar{M}\to[0,1]
such that
\eta_{A}
is supported in
\left\{\sqrt{f^{\prime}}\leq A+1\right\}
and equals to
1
on
\left\{\sqrt{f^{\prime}}\leq A\right\}
. Moreover, it holds that
|\nabla\eta_{A}|^{2}\leq 100\eta_{A}
. Applying integration by parts to (
6.64
), we obtain

\displaystyle 2\int_{\bar{M}}\eta_{A}|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\bar{M}}\eta_{A}h_{i}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}-\int_{\bar{M}}\langle\nabla\eta_{A},\nabla|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq CL^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu_{i}+2\int_{\bar{M}}|\nabla\eta_{A}||\nabla v_{i}||\nabla^{2}v_{i}|\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq CL^{\frac{1}{2}}\mu_{i}+\int_{\bar{M}}\eta_{A}^{-1}|\nabla\eta_{A}|^{2}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}+\int_{\bar{M}}\eta_{A}|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq(CL^{\frac{1}{2}}+100)\mu_{i}+\int_{\bar{M}}\eta_{A}|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}},

which, by sending
A\to\infty
, implies that
|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}
is integrable.

Thus, we can use integration by parts to (
6.64
) to obtain

\displaystyle 0\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{M}}|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}=\int_{\bar{M}}h_{i}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mu_{i}-1/2)\int_{S_{1}\cup S_{3}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}+\int_{S_{2}}h_{i}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}+\int_{S_{3}}\Phi^{\prime}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mu_{i}-1/2)\mu_{i}-(\mu_{i}-1/2)\int_{S_{2}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}+\int_{S_{2}}h_{i}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}+\int_{S_{3}}\Phi^{\prime}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(\mu_{i}-1/2)\mu_{i}+CL^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{S_{2}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}+\int_{S_{3}}\left\lvert\Phi^{\prime}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}.

(6.65)

Note that on
\bar{M}
, we have
\Delta_{f^{\prime}}\nabla v_{i}=-h_{i}(\nabla v_{i})
, thus,

\langle\Delta_{f^{\prime}}\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i}\rangle=-h_{i}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\geq-CL^{\frac{1}{2}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}

and
\Delta_{f^{\prime}}\nabla v_{i}\in L^{2}(\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}})
. Now we can use Claim
6.20
and apply
[
LZ23
, Proposition A.1]
to conclude that for any
r\in[C,L+0.4\bar{D}]
,

\displaystyle I_{\nabla v_{i}}(r):=r^{1-n}\int_{b^{\prime}=r}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}|\nabla b^{\prime}|\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{\prime}}\leq CL^{CL^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{CL^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mu_{i}\leq Ce^{CL^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mu_{i}.

Since
|\nabla b^{\prime}|
is almost 1, we obtain

\displaystyle L^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{S_{2}}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{4}\right)\mu_{i}.

(6.66)

By the standard elliptic estimate on
B(x,L^{-1})
, we obtain, for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{B(x,L^{-1}/2)}\rVert\nabla v_{i}\rVert^{2}_{C^{l,1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{l}L^{2l+n+1}\int_{B(x,L^{-1})}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{l}L^{2l+2n}\sup_{r\in[b^{\prime}(x)-L^{-1},b^{\prime}(x)+L^{-1}]}I_{\nabla v_{i}}(r)\leq C_{l}L^{2l+2n}e^{CL^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mu_{i}.

(6.67)

Combining (
6.63
) with (
6
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{S_{3}}\left\lvert\Phi^{\prime}(\nabla v_{i},\nabla v_{i})\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{8}\right)\mu_{i}.

(6.68)

Plugging (
6.66
) and (
6.68
) into (
6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{i}-1/2\geq-C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{8}\right),

which, when combined with (
6.62
), implies

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{i}-1/2\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}.

Using (
6
), we can conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{M}}|\nabla^{2}v_{i}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}\leq Ce^{-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}.

Thus, the claim is proved.

Claim 6.22
.

There exist
n-m
functions
u_{i}
such that
\int u_{i}\,\mathrm{d}V_{f^{\prime}}=0
and on
\{b\leq L+0.4\bar{D}-1\}
,

\left|\delta_{ij}-\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla u_{j}\rangle\right|+\rVert\nabla^{2}u_{i}\rVert_{C^{3}}+\left|2\langle\nabla u_{i},\nabla f^{\prime}\rangle-u_{i}\right|\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right).

(6.69)

Moreover,
\rVert u_{i}\rVert_{C^{0}}\leq Ce^{CL^{\frac{3}{2}}}
and for any
k\in[4,10^{12}n\sigma^{-1}]
,
\rVert\nabla^{2}u_{i}\rVert_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}
on
\{b^{\prime}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}-1\}
.

The proofs of (
6.69
) and the estimate for
\rVert u_{i}\rVert_{C^{0}}
are identical to the proof of
[
LZ23
, Claim 4, page 56]
. Notice that the
\{u_{1},\cdots,u_{n-m}\}
can be regarded as a Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization of
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n-m}\}
. Moreover, the constant
\theta_{2}
in
[
LZ23
, Claim 4, page 56]
can be replaced with
CL^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in our case, thanks to Claim
6.20
. For the higher estimates for
\rVert\nabla^{2}u_{i}\rVert_{C^{k}}
, by the same argument as in
[
LZ23
, Equation (5.62)]
, we have on
\{b^{\prime}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}-1\}
,

\displaystyle\rVert\nabla^{2}u_{i}\rVert_{C^{k}}\leq C_{k}L^{m_{k}}\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right)

for
k\in[4,10^{12}n\sigma^{-1}]
. Thus, if
\hat{L}
is sufficiently large, we have for
k\in[4,10^{12}n\sigma^{-1}]
,

\displaystyle\rVert\nabla^{2}u_{i}\rVert_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}

on
\{b^{\prime}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}-1\}
. This completes the proof of Claim
6.22
.

As in
[
LZ23
]
, we define

\displaystyle f_{0}:=\frac{m}{2}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{n-m}u_{i}^{2}.

Following the same proof as
[
LZ23
, Claim 5, page 58]
, we obtain

Claim 6.23
.

On
\{b^{\prime}\leq L+0.4\bar{D}-2\}
,

\left|f^{\prime}-f_{0}\right|\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right),

and for any
k\in[1,10^{12}n\sigma^{-1}]
,
\rVert f^{\prime}-f_{0}\rVert_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}
.

Now, we can follow the same argument as in
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.3]
to conclude the proof. For readers’ convenience, we sketch the rest of the proof as follows.

We set
N_{0}:=\{f_{0}=m/2\}
and let
\kappa_{i}^{t}
be the family of diffeomorphisms generated by
\nabla u_{i}
. We define the map
\psi_{4}:\mathbb{R}^{n-m}\times N_{0}\to\bar{M}
by

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(\vec{x},w)=\kappa_{1}^{x_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\kappa_{n-m}^{x_{n-m}}(w),

where
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-m})\in\mathbb{R}^{n-m}
and
w\in N_{0}
. Then it follows from Claim
6.22
that
\psi_{4}
is a diffeomorphism from
B_{g_{E}}(0,L+0.3\bar{D})\times N_{0}
onto its image and it satisfies

\displaystyle\|\psi_{4}^{*}g^{\prime}-g_{E}\times g_{N_{0}}\|_{C^{3}}\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right),

where
g_{N_{0}}
is the restriction of
g^{\prime}
on
N_{0}
, and for any
k\in[4,10^{11}n\sigma^{-1}]
,

\displaystyle\|\psi_{4}^{*}g^{\prime}-g_{E}\times g_{N_{0}}\|_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}.

In addition, we can define a diffeomorphism
\phi:S^{m}\to N_{0}
(cf.
[
LZ23
, Equations (5.82) and (5.83)]
) so that

\displaystyle\|\phi^{*}g_{N_{0}}-g_{S^{m}}\|_{C^{2,1/2}}\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right)

and for any
k\in[3,10^{11}n\sigma^{-1}]
,

\displaystyle\|\phi^{*}g_{N_{0}}-g_{S^{m}}\|_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}.

Finally, we define a map
\psi_{5}:B_{g_{E}}(0,L+0.3\bar{D})\times S^{m}\to M
by

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(\vec{x},w):=\psi_{3}\circ\psi_{4}(\vec{x},\phi(w))

where
\vec{x}\in B_{g_{E}}(0,L+0.3\bar{D})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-m}
and
w\in S^{m}
. By our construction, it is not hard to show that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\psi_{5}^{*}g-\bar{g}\right\rvert\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right)

(6.70)

and for any
k\in[1,10^{11}n\sigma^{-1}]
,

\displaystyle\|\psi_{5}^{*}g-\bar{g}\|_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}.

(6.71)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\psi_{5}^{*}f-\bar{f}\right\rvert\leq C\exp\left(CL^{\frac{3}{2}}-\frac{L^{2}}{32}\right)

(6.72)

and for any
k\in[1,10^{11}n\sigma^{-1}]
,

\displaystyle\|\psi_{5}^{*}f-\bar{f}\|_{C^{k}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}}.

(6.73)

On the other hand, we claim that

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L\}}\left|\psi_{5}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq e^{-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.2\bar{D})^{2}}{4-3\sigma}}.

(6.74)

Indeed, by our definition of
L
, (
6.71
), (
6.73
) and (
6.50
) in Corollary
6.17
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{5}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}}\leq C\left|\psi_{5}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}_{\psi_{5}^{*}g}\leq Ce^{\frac{\psi_{5}^{*}f}{2}-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D})^{2}}{8(1-\sigma)}}\leq Ce^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D})^{2}}{8(1-\sigma)}}

on
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
. Thus, we can estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L\}}\left|\psi_{5}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq C\left\lvert\{\bar{b}\leq L\}\right\rvert e^{-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D})^{2}}{4(1-\sigma)}}\leq e^{-\frac{(\textbf{r}_{A}+0.5\bar{D})^{2}}{4-3\sigma}},

provided that
\hat{L}
is sufficiently large.

Moreover, we obtain from (
6.71
) and (
6.73
) that

\displaystyle\int_{\{L\leq\bar{b}\leq L+0.2\bar{D}\}}\left|\psi_{5}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}_{\bar{g}}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq Ce^{-\frac{2L^{2}}{33}-\frac{L^{2}}{4}}.

(6.75)

From (
6.74
) and (
6.75
), we conclude that (
5.2
) holds for
\psi_{5}
with radius
L+0.2\bar{D}
.

Combining this with (
6.70
), (
6.71
), (
6.72
) and (
6.73
), we conclude that
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\geq L+0.2\bar{D}
by using the diffeomorphism
\psi_{5}
, if
\hat{L}
is sufficiently large. By our definition of
L
and
\bar{D}
, it follows that
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\geq\textbf{r}_{A}+0.1\bar{D}
.

In sum, the proof of the theorem is complete.
∎

By using Proposition
5.2
and Theorem
6.18
iteratively, we obtain the following result. Note that by Theorem
2.13
(i), the assumption (
5.1
) holds for a constant
C_{V}=C_{V}(n,Y)
.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Assume
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},0)\in\mathcal{X}
and
[-10,0]\subset I
. For any small
\sigma\in(0,1/10)
, there exists a constant
L=L(n,Y,\sigma)
such that if the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,g(-1),f_{x_{0}^{*}}(-1)\right)
satisfies
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq L
, then

\displaystyle\min\{\mathbf{r}_{A},\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\}\geq(1-3\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}.

Proof.

We choose
L:=100\max\{L_{1}(n,C_{V},\sigma)=L_{1}(n,Y,\sigma),\hat{L}(n,Y,\sigma),\sigma^{-1}\}
, where
L_{1}
and
\hat{L}
are from Proposition
5.2
and Theorem
6.18
, respectively.

By Theorem
6.18
, if
L\leq\textbf{r}_{A}\leq(1-2\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}
, then
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\geq\textbf{r}_{A}+\bar{D}_{100}/10
.
By Proposition
5.2
, we obtain
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}-2
. If
(1-2\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}<L
, the conclusion obvious holds. Otherwise, we can use Proposition
5.2
and Theorem
6.18
iteratively to conclude that

\displaystyle\min\{\mathbf{r}_{A},\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\}\geq(1-2\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}-2\geq(1-3\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}.

∎

By the same argument, we also have:

Theorem 6.25
.

Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be the completion of a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-10,0)}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Fix
z\in Z_{0}
. For any small
\sigma\in(0,1/10)
, there exists a constant
L=L(n,Y,\sigma)
such that if the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,g(-1),f_{z}(-1)\right)
satisfies
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq L
, then

\displaystyle\min\{\mathbf{r}_{A},\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\}\geq(1-3\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}.

Lojasiewicz inequality and summability of entropy gaps

Combining Theorem
5.4
and Theorem
6.24
, we obtain the desired Lojasiewicz inequality.

Theorem 6.26

(Lojasiewicz inequality)
.

For any
\beta\in(0,3/4)
, there exist constants
C=C(n,Y)
and
L_{\beta}=L(n,Y,\beta)
such that the following property holds.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Assume
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and
[t_{0}-10r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
. If the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,r^{-2}g(t_{0}-r^{2}),f_{x_{0}^{*}}(t_{0}-r^{2})\right)
satisfies
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq L_{\beta}
, then

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2})-\Theta_{m}\right|\leq C\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2}/2)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2r^{2})\right)^{\beta}.

Proof.

By translation and rescaling, we may assume
t_{0}=0
and
r=1
.

If
\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/2)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2)\geq 1
, the conclusion obviously holds. Thus, we assume
\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/2)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2)<1
so that
\mathbf{r}_{E}
is well-defined.

Thus, it follows from Theorems
5.4
and
6.24
that for
\sigma>0
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1)-\Theta_{m}\right|\leq C(n,Y)\exp\left(-\frac{3\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{2}}{16}\right)\leq C(n,Y)\exp\left(-\frac{3(1-3\sigma)^{2}\mathbf{r}_{E}^{2}}{16}\right),

(6.76)

provided that
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq L(n,Y,\sigma)
. By Definition
6.9
, (
6.76
) implies

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1)-\Theta_{m}\right|\leq C(n,Y)\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1/2)-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(2)\right)^{\frac{3(1-3\sigma)^{2}}{4}}.

This completes the proof by choosing
\beta=3(1-3\sigma)^{2}/4
.
∎

Similarly, from Theorem
6.25
, we have:

Theorem 6.27
.

For any
\beta\in(0,3/4)
, there exist constants
C=C(n,Y)
and
L_{\beta}=L(n,Y,\beta)
such that the following property holds. Let
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
be the completion of a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-10r^{2},0)}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Fix
z\in Z_{0}
. If the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,r^{-2}g(-r^{2}),f_{z}(-r^{2})\right)
satisfies
\textbf{r}_{A}\geq L_{\beta}
, then

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{z}(r^{2})-\Theta_{m}\right|\leq C\left(\mathcal{W}_{z}(r^{2}/2)-\mathcal{W}_{z}(2r^{2})\right)^{\beta}.

As a corollary, we have the following summability result. For simplicity, we only state it for smooth closed Ricci flows. The corresponding generalization to noncollapsed Ricci flow limit spaces will be proved in
[
FL25b
]
.

Corollary 6.28
.

For any small
0<\epsilon\leq\epsilon(n,Y)
,
\alpha\in(1/4,1)
and
\delta\leq\delta(n,Y,\epsilon,\alpha)
, the following holds. Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Fix
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and constants
s_{2}>s_{1}>0
. If

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(s_{1})-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(s_{2})\right|<\delta,

(6.77)

and, for any
s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
,
x_{0}^{*}
is
(n-m,\delta,\sqrt{s})
-cylindrical (see Definition
2.26
), then

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}\leq r_{j}=2^{-j}\leq s_{2}}\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j-1})\right|^{\alpha}<\epsilon.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
. For a given
\alpha\in(1/4,1)
, we choose constants
\theta\in(1/3,1)
and
q
such that

\displaystyle\max\left\{1,\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\right\}<q<\theta^{-1}.

(6.78)

Notice that by our assumption on
\alpha
, such choices are possible.

By assumption, for any
s\in[s_{1},s_{2}]
,
x_{0}^{*}
is
(n-m,\delta,\sqrt{s})
-cylindrical (see (
2.8
)). Thus, if
\delta\leq\epsilon(n,Y,\theta)
, it follows from Lemma
2.27
that the weighted Riemannian manifold
(M,s^{-1}g(-s),f(-s))
satisfies
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}\geq L_{\beta}
, where
\beta:=1/(1+\theta)
,
\sigma
is defined by
\beta=3(1-3\sigma)^{2}/4
, and
L_{\beta}
is the same constant in Theorem
6.26
. Thus, for any
j
with
s_{1}\leq r_{j}\leq s_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\Theta_{m}\right|^{1+\theta}\leq C(n,Y)\left(\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j+1})-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j-1})\right).

Define
N
to be the largest number so that
r_{N}\geq s_{1}
. Similarly, define
N^{\prime}
to be the smallest number so that
r_{N^{\prime}}\leq s_{2}
. Moreover, we set
j_{0}\in[N^{\prime},N]
to be the largest number such that
\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})\leq\Theta_{m}
. If no such
j_{0}
exists, we set
j_{0}=N^{\prime}
.

By the proof of
[
FL25c
, Claim 3.3]
(see also
[
CMI15
, Lemma 6.9]
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\Theta_{m}|\leq C(n,Y,\theta)(j+1-N^{\prime})^{-\frac{1}{\theta}}

(6.79)

for
j\in[N^{\prime},j_{0}]
and

\displaystyle|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\Theta_{m}|\leq C(n,Y,\theta)(N+1-j)^{-\frac{1}{\theta}}

(6.80)

for
j\in[j_{0}+1,N]
.

Now we set
b_{j}:=\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j-1})
and
a_{j}=|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r_{j})-\Theta_{m}|
. Moreover, we choose a large number
k\in\mathbb{N}
to be determined later.

By a direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}}b_{l}(l-N^{\prime})^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}}(a_{l-1}-a_{l})(l-N^{\prime})^{q}

\displaystyle=a_{N^{\prime}+k-1}k^{q}-a_{j_{0}}(j_{0}-N^{\prime})^{q}+\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}-1}a_{l}\left((l+1-N^{\prime})^{q}-(l-N^{\prime})^{q}\right)

\displaystyle\leq a_{N^{\prime}+k-1}k^{q}+q\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}-1}a_{l}(l+1-N^{\prime})^{q-1}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y,\theta)\left(k^{q-\theta^{-1}}+q\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}-1}(l+1-N^{\prime})^{q-1-\theta^{-1}}\right)\leq\Psi(k^{-1}),

(6.81)

where we used (
6.79
) for the last line. Similarly, by using (
6.80
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{l=j_{0}+1}^{N-k}b_{l}(N-l)^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=j_{0}+1}^{N-k}(a_{l}-a_{l-1})(N-l)^{q}

\displaystyle=a_{N-k}k^{q}-a_{j_{0}}(N-j_{0})^{q}+\sum_{l=j_{0}+1}^{N-k-1}a_{l}\left((N+1-l)^{q}-(N-l)^{q}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y,\theta)\left(k^{q-\theta^{-1}}+q\sum_{l=j_{0}+1}^{N-k-1}(N-l+1)^{q-1-\theta^{-1}}\right)\leq\Psi(k^{-1}).

Now, we conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}}b_{l}^{\alpha}=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}}\left(b_{l}^{\alpha}(l-N^{\prime})^{\alpha q}\right)(l-N^{\prime})^{-\alpha q}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}}b_{l}(l-N^{\prime})^{q}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\sum_{l=N^{\prime}+k}^{j_{0}}l^{-\frac{\alpha q}{1-\alpha}}\right)^{1-\alpha}\leq\Psi(k^{-1}),

where the last inequality holds by our choice (
6.78
) and (
6.81
).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{l=j_{0}+1}^{N-k}b_{l}^{\alpha}\leq\Psi(k^{-1}).

Combining all these estimates with (
6.77
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{l=N^{\prime}}^{N}b_{l}^{\alpha}\leq\Psi(k^{-1})+2k\delta.

Thus, we can first choose a large
k
and then choose a small
\delta
such that the sum above is bounded by
\epsilon
.

In sum, the proof is complete.
∎

7
Strong uniqueness for cylindrical tangent flows

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in[-T,0)}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
, where
0
is the first singular time. Suppose
(Z,d_{Z},\mathfrak{t})
is the completion of
\mathcal{X}
(see Subsection
2
). Fix a point
z\in Z_{0}
and consider the modified Ricci flow
(M,g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
with respect to
z
(see Definition
2.21
).

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose
z
is a cylindrical singularity (see Definition
2.22
). Denote by
\textbf{r}_{A}(s)
and
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)
the radius functions in Definition
5.1
with respect to
(M,g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
. Then, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\min\{\textbf{r}_{A}(s),\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)\}\geq\sqrt{(12-\epsilon)\log s},

where
\sigma=10^{-3}\epsilon
, provided that
s
is sufficiently large.

Proof.

We set
W(s):=\mathcal{W}(g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
. By Theorem
2.23
, any tangent flow at
z
is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
. In particular, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{s\to+\infty}W(s)=\Theta_{n-k}.

Moreover, for any
L>1
, we have

\displaystyle\min\{\textbf{r}_{A}(s),\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)\}\geq L,

if
s
is sufficiently large. By Theorem
6.27
, this implies for
\beta=3(1-3\sigma)^{2}/4
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert W(s)-\Theta_{n-k}\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y)\left(W(s+\log 2)-W(s-\log 2)\right)^{\beta}

for sufficiently large
s
. By the same argument as in the proof of Corollary
6.28
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left\lvert W(s)-\Theta_{n-k}\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)s^{-\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}}=C(n,Y,\epsilon)s^{-\frac{3(1-3\sigma)^{2}}{4-3(1-3\sigma)^{2}}}

for large
s
, which implies

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{\mathbf{r}_{E}^{2}(s)}{4}\right):=\left\lvert W(s+\log 2)-W(s-\log 2)\right\rvert\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)s^{-\frac{3(1-3\sigma)^{2}}{4-3(1-3\sigma)^{2}}}.

(7.1)

By Theorem
6.25
and (
7.1
), it follows that

\displaystyle\min\{\textbf{r}_{A}(s),\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)\}\geq(1-3\sigma)\sqrt{\frac{12(1-3\sigma)^{2}}{4-3(1-3\sigma)^{2}}\log s-C(n,Y,\epsilon)}.

Thus, the conclusion holds by our choice of
\sigma
, if
s
is sufficiently large. ∎

Based on Theorem
7.1
, we prove the following strong uniqueness result.

Theorem 7.2

(Strong uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow I)
.

Suppose
z
is a cylindrical singularity. Then for any small
\epsilon>0
, there exists a large constant
\bar{s}
such that for any
s_{0}\geq\bar{s}
, there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{s_{0}}
from
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
onto a subset of
M
such that on
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
and for all
s\geq s_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\left[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}g^{z}(s)-\bar{g}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+\left[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}f^{z}(s)-\bar{f}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

Here, the
C^{[\epsilon^{-1}]}
-norms are with respect to
\bar{g}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we fix a small constant
\epsilon>0
and set
\sigma=10^{-3}\epsilon
.

Let
\textbf{r}_{A}(s)
and
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)
denote the radius functions defined in Definition
5.1
with respect to
(M,g^{z}(s),f^{z}(s))
, and let
\mathbf{r}_{E}(s)
be defined as in Definition
6.9
. By Theorem
7.1
, for sufficiently large
s
, we have

\displaystyle\min\{\textbf{r}_{A}(s),\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)\}\geq\sqrt{(12-\epsilon)\log s}.

(7.2)

Let
\varphi_{s}
be the diffeomorphism corresponding to
\textbf{r}_{A}(s)
in Definition
5.1
. Then by Corollary
6.17
and its proof, for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
, on the set
\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon/3)\log s}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left[\varphi_{s}^{*}\left(\frac{g^{z}(s)}{2}-\nabla^{2}f^{z}(s)-\mathrm{Ric}(g^{z}(s))\right)\right]_{l}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon,l)\exp\left(\frac{\bar{f}}{2}-\frac{\textbf{r}_{A}(s)^{2}}{8}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon,l)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{8}},

provided
s
is sufficiently large. Here, we have used (
7.2
) for the last inequality.

Next, we set
\Omega_{s}:=\varphi_{s}\left(\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon/3)\log s}\right\}\right)
. Then for sufficiently large
s
, we have on
\Omega_{s}
,

\displaystyle\left[\frac{g^{z}(s)}{2}-\nabla^{2}f^{z}(s)-\mathrm{Ric}(g^{z}(s))\right]_{l}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon,l)e^{\frac{\bar{f}\circ\varphi^{-1}_{s}}{2}}s^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{8}}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon,l)s^{-1-\frac{\epsilon}{24}},

(7.3)

where the norm is taken with respect to
g^{z}(s)
. Taking the trace yields, on
\Omega_{s}
,

\displaystyle\left[\frac{n}{2}-\Delta f^{z}(s)-\mathrm{R}(g^{z}(s))\right]_{l}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon,l)e^{\frac{\bar{f}\circ\varphi^{-1}_{s}}{2}}s^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{8}}\leq C_{1}(n,Y,\epsilon,l)s^{-1-\frac{\epsilon}{24}}.

(7.4)

By Definition
5.1
, any
H_{n}
-center of
(4\pi)^{-n/2}e^{-f^{z}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(s)}
lies in
\varphi_{s}\left(\{\bar{b}\leq C(n)\}\right)
. Therefore, for sufficiently large
s
, it follows from Theorem
2.13
(ii) that any
x\notin\Omega_{s}
satisfies

\displaystyle f^{z}(x,s)\geq(1-\epsilon/2.5)\log s.

(7.5)

Now we choose
\bar{s}
sufficiently large such that (
7.5
) holds for any
s\geq\bar{s}
and

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{s}}^{\infty}C_{1}s^{-1-\frac{\epsilon}{24}}\,\mathrm{d}s<1,

where
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,Y,\epsilon,[\epsilon^{-1}])
is the constant from (
7.4
), and both (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) hold for
l=[\epsilon^{-1}]
and all
s\geq\bar{s}
. For the rest of the proof, we fix
s_{0}\geq\bar{s}
.

Claim 7.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\Omega^{s_{0}}:=\varphi_{s_{0}}\left(\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon/2)\log s_{0}}\right\}\right)\subset\Omega_{s},\quad\forall s\geq s_{0}.

Moreover, for any
x\in\Omega^{s_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\left|f^{z}(x,s_{0})-f^{z}(x,s)\right|\leq 1,\quad\forall s\geq s_{0}.

(7.6)

Fix
x\in\Omega^{s_{0}}
, and suppose
s_{1}>s_{0}
is the maximal time
s
such that
x\in\Omega_{s}
. If
s_{1}<\infty
, then by (
7.4
) and the evolution equation for
f^{z}
(see (
2.7
)), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|f^{z}(x,s_{0})-f^{z}(x,s_{1})\right|\leq\int_{s_{0}}^{\infty}C_{1}s^{-1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s<1.

(7.7)

By the definition of
\textbf{r}_{A}
, we then have

f^{z}(x,s_{1})\leq(1-\epsilon/2)\log s_{0}+2<(1-\epsilon/2.5)\log s_{1},

which, by (
7.5
), implies that
x
lies in the interior of
\Omega_{s_{1}}
, contradicting the maximality of
s_{1}
. Hence
s_{1}=\infty
, and (
7.6
) follows from (
7.7
). This completes the proof of Claim
7.3
.

By Claim
7.3
, we conclude that
\Omega^{s_{0}}\subset\Omega_{s}
for any
s\geq s_{0}
, so the estimates (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) hold on
\Omega^{s_{0}}
. Since the right-hand sides of these estimates are integrable in
s
, it follows from the evolution equation (
2.7
) that on
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon/2)\log s_{0}}\right\}
,
\left(\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}g^{z}(s),\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}f^{z}(s)\right)
converges smoothly to
\left(g_{\infty},f_{\infty}\right)
as
s\to\infty
.

Moreover, by integration, we obtain for all
s\geq s_{0}
,

\displaystyle[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}g^{z}(s)-g_{\infty}]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}f^{z}(s)-f_{\infty}]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}\int_{s}^{\infty}C(n,Y,\epsilon)t^{-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{8}}\,dt\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{8}}.

(7.8)

Claim 7.4
.

There exists a self-diffeomorphism
\varphi
of
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
such that

\displaystyle g_{\infty}=\varphi^{*}\bar{g},\quad f_{\infty}=\varphi^{*}\bar{f}.

By Lemma
2.27
, we have for a sequence
s_{i}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left(M,g^{z}(s_{i}),f^{z}(s_{i}),p_{i}\right)\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\text{pointed-Cheeger--Gromov}\hskip 8.19447pt}\left(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f},\bar{p}\right).

Here
p_{i}
and
\bar{p}
are minimum points of
f^{z}(s_{i})
and
\bar{f}
, respectively. Hence, for sufficiently large
i
, there exists a diffeomorphism
\psi_{i}:U_{i}\subset\bar{M}\to M
, such that
U_{i}\subset U_{i+1}
, and
\bigcup_{i}U_{i}=\bar{M}
, satisfying

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(\bar{p})=p_{i},\quad\psi_{i}^{*}g^{z}(s_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}}\bar{g},\quad\psi_{i}^{*}f^{z}(s_{i})\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\infty}}\bar{f}.

(7.9)

From (
7.9
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{s_{0}}\right)^{*}\bar{g}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}g_{\infty}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{s_{0}}\right)^{*}\bar{f}\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}f_{\infty}.

Therefore, by passing to a subsequence, the maps
\psi_{i}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{s_{0}}
converge smoothly to a diffeomorphism
\varphi
from
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
onto itself satisfying
g_{\infty}=\varphi^{*}\bar{g}
and
f_{\infty}=\varphi^{*}\bar{f}
. This completes the proof of Claim
7.4
.

If we replace
\varphi_{s_{0}}
by
\varphi_{s_{0}}\circ\varphi^{-1}
(still denoted by
\varphi_{s_{0}}
), then combining (
7
) with Claim
7.4
yields

\displaystyle\left[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}g^{z}(s)-\bar{g}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+\left[\varphi_{s_{0}}^{*}f^{z}(s)-\bar{f}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{8}}.

for all
s\geq s_{0}
, on
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{0}}\right\}
. This completes the proof after adjusting constants.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

In the same setting of Theorem
7.2
, for any
s_{2}>s_{1}\geq\bar{s}
, there exists an isometry
\varphi_{s_{2},s_{1}}:(\bar{M},\bar{g})\to(\bar{M},\bar{g})
such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{s_{2},s_{1}}^{*}\bar{f}=\bar{f},\quad\varphi_{s_{2}}\circ\varphi_{s_{2},s_{1}}=\varphi_{s_{1}}

on
\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{1}}\right\}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.2
, we know for
\Omega:=\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log s_{1}}\right\}
,

\left(\Omega,\varphi_{s_{1}}^{*}g^{z}(s),\varphi_{s_{1}}^{*}f^{z}(s)\right)\xrightarrow[s\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}\left(\Omega,\bar{g},\bar{f}\right),

and

\left(\Omega,\varphi_{s_{2}}^{*}g^{z}(s),\varphi_{s_{2}}^{*}f^{z}(s)\right)\xrightarrow[s\to\infty]{C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}}\left(\Omega,\bar{g},\bar{f}\right).

Therefore,
\varphi:=\varphi_{s_{2}}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{s_{1}}
is well-defined on
\Omega
such that

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\bar{g}=\varphi_{s_{1}}^{*}\circ\left(\varphi_{s_{2}}^{-1}\right)^{*}\bar{g}=\lim_{s\to\infty}\varphi_{s_{1}}^{*}\circ\left(\varphi_{s_{2}}^{-1}\right)^{*}\circ\varphi_{s_{2}}^{*}g^{z}(s)=\lim_{s\to\infty}\varphi_{s_{1}}^{*}g^{z}(s)=\bar{g}

(7.10)

and similarly

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\bar{f}=\bar{f}.

(7.11)

By (
7.10
) and (
7.11
),
\varphi
is an isometry of
\Omega
, which preserves
\bar{f}
. Thus,
\varphi
is the restriction of a map
\varphi_{s_{2},s_{1}}
in
\mathrm{O}(n-m)\times\mathrm{Iso}(S^{m})
, which is an isometry of
(\bar{M},\bar{g})
preserving
\bar{f}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 7.6

(Strong uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow II)
.

Suppose
z
is a cylindrical singularity. Then for any small
\epsilon>0
, there exists a large constant
\bar{s}
such that for any
j\geq\bar{s}
, there exists a diffeomorphism
\psi_{j}
from
\Omega^{j}:=\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log j}\right\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\psi_{j+1}=\psi_{j}\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\Omega^{j},

and for all
s\geq j
, we have on
\Omega^{j}
,

\displaystyle\left[\psi_{j}^{*}g^{z}(s)-\bar{g}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+\left[\psi_{j}^{*}f^{z}(s)-\bar{f}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}s^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

Proof.

We can define
\psi_{j}
inductively. First, we choose
j_{0}
with
j_{0}-1<\bar{s}\leq j_{0}
and set
\psi_{j_{0}}=\varphi_{j_{0}}
as in Theorem
7.2
. Once
\psi_{j}
is defined, we can apply Lemma
7.5
to obtain an isometry
\varphi
of
(\bar{M},\bar{g})
so that on
\Omega^{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}\bar{f}=\bar{f},\quad\varphi_{j+1}\circ\varphi=\psi_{j}.

Then, we set
\psi_{j+1}:=\varphi_{j+1}\circ\varphi
, which is well-defined on
\Omega^{j+1}
. Thus, the conclusion follows from the induction and Theorem
7.2
.
∎

Our results also apply to complete ancient solutions to Ricci flow with cylindrical tangent flow at infinity (see
[
FL25a
, Example 11.5]
). First, we have the following definition.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in(-\infty,0]}\}
be a complete Ricci flow with bounded curvature on any compact time interval of
(-\infty,0]
and with entropy bounded below.
\mathcal{X}
is said to have a cylindrical tangent flow at infinity if one of the tangent flows at infinity is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

The following result is parallel to Theorem
2.23
, which is proved by the same method using
[
LZ23
, Theorem 6.5]
.

Theorem 7.8

(Uniqueness of the cylindrical tangent flow at infinity)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in(-\infty,0]}\}
be a complete Ricci flow with bounded curvature on any compact time interval of
(-\infty,0]
and with entropy bounded below. If
\mathcal{X}
has
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
as a tangent flow at infinity, then any tangent flow at infinity is isometric to
\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{k}
.

Now, we fix a base point
p^{*}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
and set
\mathrm{d}\nu_{p^{*};t}=(4\pi|t|)^{-n/2}e^{-f(t)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)}
. Let
\phi_{t}
be the family of diffeomorphisms generated by
-\nabla_{g(t)}f(t)
with
\phi_{0}=\mathrm{id}
. Then we define

\begin{cases}&g^{\prime}(s):=e^{s}\phi^{*}_{-e^{-s}}g(-e^{-s}),\\
&f^{\prime}(s):=\phi^{*}_{-e^{-s}}f_{z}(-e^{-s}).\end{cases}

(7.12)

Clearly,
(g^{\prime}(s),f^{\prime}(s))
satisfies (
2.7
). Similar to Theorem
7.1
and Theorem
7.6
, we have the following results by the same proof.

Theorem 7.9
.

Denote by
\textbf{r}_{A}(s)
and
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)
the radius functions in Definition
5.1
with respect to
(M,g^{\prime}(s),f^{\prime}(s))
. Under the same assumptions of Theorem
7.8
, for any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\min\{\textbf{r}_{A}(s),\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}(s)\}\geq\sqrt{(12-\epsilon)\log|s|},

where
\sigma=10^{-3}\epsilon
, provided that
|s|
is sufficiently large.

Theorem 7.10

(Strong uniqueness of cylindrical tangent flow at infinity)
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
7.8
, for any small
\epsilon>0
, there exists a large constant
\bar{s}
such that for any
j\geq\bar{s}
, there exists a diffeomorphism
\psi_{-j}
from
\Omega^{j}=\left\{\bar{b}\leq 2\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)\log j}\right\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\psi_{-j-1}=\psi_{-j}\quad\mathrm{on}\quad\Omega^{j},

and for any
s\leq-j
, we have on
\Omega^{j}
,

\displaystyle\left[\psi_{-j}^{*}g^{\prime}(s)-\bar{g}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}+\left[\psi_{-j}^{*}f^{\prime}(s)-\bar{f}\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{2}}|s|^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

8
Generalization to quotient cylindrical geometries

In this subsection, we prove the Lojasiewicz inequality near quotient cylinders. First, we set as before

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1}=(\bar{M},\bar{g},\bar{f})=\left(\mathbb{R}^{n-m}\times S^{m},g_{E}\times g_{S^{m}},\frac{|\vec{x}|^{2}}{4}+\frac{m}{2}+\Theta_{m}\right).

For any finite group
\Gamma\leqslant\mathrm{Iso}(\mathcal{C}^{n-m}_{-1})
acting freely on
\bar{M}
, we set
\pi:\bar{M}\to\bar{M}/\Gamma
to be the natural quotient map and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{n-m}(\Gamma)_{-1}=(\bar{M}_{\Gamma},\bar{g}_{\Gamma},\bar{f}_{\Gamma}),

(8.1)

where
\bar{M}_{\Gamma}=\bar{M}/\Gamma
,
\bar{g}_{\Gamma}
is the quotient metric of
\bar{g}
, and
\bar{f}_{\Gamma}
is defined so that

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\bar{f}_{\Gamma}=\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|.

It is clear by a direct calculation that

\displaystyle\Theta_{m}(\Gamma):=\mathcal{W}(\bar{g}_{\Gamma},\bar{f}_{\Gamma})=\Theta_{m}-\log|\Gamma|.

Similar to Definition
5.1
, we have the following definitions. As before, we set
\bar{b}=2\sqrt{|\bar{f}|}
and
b=2\sqrt{|f|}
.

Definition 8.1
.

For
\sigma\in(0,1/10)
and a weighted Riemannian manifold
(M,g,f)
with
\Phi=\mathbf{\Phi}(g,f)=\dfrac{g}{2}-\mathrm{Ric}(g)-\nabla^{2}f
, we define

(A)

(
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}
-radius)

\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}
to be the largest number
L
for which there exists a smooth covering map
\varphi_{A}
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{A}^{*}g\right]_{5}+\left[\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{A}^{*}f\right]_{5}\leq e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{L^{2}}{16}}.

(B)

(
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}
-radius)

\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}
to be the largest number
L
for which there exists a smooth covering map
\varphi_{B}
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g\right]_{0}+\left[\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{B}^{*}f\right]_{0}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}},

and

\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L\}}\left|\varphi_{B}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4-\sigma}}.

Furthermore, for all
k\in[1,10^{10}n\sigma^{-1}]
, the
C^{k}
-norms of
\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g
and
\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{B}^{*}f
are bounded by
1
.

Similar to Proposition
5.2
, we have the following proposition; see also
[
LZ23
, Theorem 5.6]
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
(M,g,f)
be a normalized weighted Riemannian manifold such that

\displaystyle\int_{M\setminus B(p,L)}1\,\mathrm{d}V_{f}\leq C_{V}e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{15}},\quad\forall L>1,

(8.2)

where
p
is a fixed minimum point of
f
. Then there exists a constant
L_{1}=L_{1}(n,C_{V},|\Gamma|,\sigma)
such that if
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}\geq L_{1}
for
(M,g,f)
, then

\displaystyle\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}\geq\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}-3.

Proof.

We set
L=\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}\geq L_{1}
. By the definition, there exists a smooth covering map
\varphi_{B}
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g\right]_{0}+\left[\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{B}^{*}f\right]_{0}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}},

and for
\Phi=g/2-\mathrm{Ric}(g)-\nabla^{2}f
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L\}}\left|\varphi_{B}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4-\sigma}}.

Furthermore, all
C^{k}
-norms of
\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g
and
\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{B}^{*}f
are bounded by
1
for
k\in[1,10^{10}n\sigma^{-1}]
. It follows from the assumption (
8.2
) that

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L-1/3\}\setminus B_{\varphi_{B}^{*}g}(p^{\prime},D)}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\varphi_{B}^{*}f}\leq C(n,C_{V},|\Gamma|)e^{-\frac{D^{2}}{15.1}},\quad\forall D>1,

(8.3)

where
p^{\prime}
is a fixed point in
\{\varphi_{B}^{-1}(p)\}
. Indeed, by the definition of
\textbf{r}_{B}^{\Gamma}
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{B}^{-1}(B(p,D))\subset B_{\varphi_{B}^{*}g}(p^{\prime},D+C(n)).

Thus, (
8.3
) follows from (
8.2
) by adjusting
D
.

By Proposition
5.2
(see (
5.18
) and Remark
5.3
), we can find a self-diffeomorphism
\psi_{1}
of
\{\bar{b}\leq L-1\}
such that

\displaystyle\left[\psi_{1}^{*}\varphi_{B}^{*}g-\bar{g}\right]_{5}+\left[\psi_{1}^{*}\varphi_{B}^{*}f-\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|\right]_{5}\leq e^{\frac{\bar{f}}{4}-\frac{(L-2)^{2}}{16}}

on
\{\bar{b}\leq L-2\}
. We denote the submersion
\varphi_{B}\circ\psi_{1}
by
\varphi_{A}
.

Then we consider the subset
\{b\leq L-5/2\}\bigcap\varphi_{B}(U)
and set
U^{\prime}:=\psi_{1}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{B}^{-1}\left(\{b\leq L-5/2\}\bigcap\varphi_{B}(U)\right)
. Since
\psi_{1}
is almost an identity, it is clear by the definition of
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}
that

\displaystyle\{\bar{b}\leq L-3\}\subset U^{\prime}\subset\{\bar{b}\leq L-2\}.

From our construction, the map
\varphi_{A}
from
U^{\prime}
is a covering map of degree
|\Gamma|
. Consequently,
\varphi_{A}
satisfies the conclusions for
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}
in Definition
8.1
, and hence we have

\displaystyle\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}\geq\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}-3.

∎

Similar to Theorem
5.4
, we next prove

Theorem 8.3
.

There exist constants
L_{2}=L_{2}(n,Y,|\Gamma|)>0
and
C=C(n,Y)>0
such that the following holds.
Let
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
be a closed Ricci flow with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Assume
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and
[t_{0}-2r^{2},t_{0}]\subset I
. If the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,r^{-2}g(t_{0}-r^{2}),f_{x_{0}^{*}}(t_{0}-r^{2})\right)
satisfies
\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}\geq L_{2}
, then

\left|\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(r^{2})-\Theta_{m}(\Gamma)\right|\leq C\exp\left(-\frac{3(\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|})^{2}}{16}\right).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
t_{0}=0
,
r=1
and choose a small parameter
\epsilon\ll 1
to be determined later. Also, we set
L=\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}
,
g_{0}=g(-1)
and
f_{0}=f_{x_{0}^{*}}(-1)
for simplicity.

By Definition
8.1
, we can find a smooth covering map
\varphi_{B}
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
onto a subset of
M
such that

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g_{0}\right]_{0}+\left[\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{B}^{*}f_{0}\right]_{0}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{33}},

and

\displaystyle\int_{\{\bar{b}\leq L\}}\left|\varphi_{B}^{*}\Phi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}V_{\bar{f}}\leq e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{4-\sigma}},

where
\Phi=g_{0}/2-\mathrm{Ric}\left(g_{0}\right)-\nabla^{2}f_{0}
. Furthermore, all
C^{k}
-norms of
\bar{g}-\varphi_{B}^{*}g_{0}
and
\bar{f}-\log|\Gamma|-\varphi_{B}^{*}f_{0}
are bounded by
1
for
k\in[1,10^{10}n\sigma^{-1}]
. By Theorem
2.13
(i), the assumption (
8.2
) holds for
C_{V}=C_{V}(n,Y)
. Now we choose
L_{2}=L_{1}(n,C_{V},|\Gamma|)
, where
L_{1}
is the same constant as in Proposition
8.2
so that it applies to the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,g(-1),f(-1)\right)
.

Choose a cut-off function
\eta
on
\bar{M}
such that
\eta=0
outside
\{\bar{b}<L-1\}
and
\eta=1
on
\{\bar{b}<L-2\}
. Let
g_{1}=\bar{g}+\eta(\varphi_{B}^{*}g_{0}-\bar{g}),f_{1}=\bar{f}+\eta(\varphi_{B}^{*}f_{0}-\bar{f}+\log|\Gamma|)
. Then it follows from the proof of Theorem
5.4
that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}(g_{1},f_{1})-\Theta_{m}\right|\leq C(n,Y)e^{-\frac{3L^{2}}{16}}.

By Proposition
3.1
, we have

\left|\mathcal{W}(g_{0},f_{0})-\mathcal{W}(g_{1},f_{1})+\log|\Gamma|\right|\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{-\frac{(1-\epsilon)L^{2}}{4}}.

Combining these estimates, we conclude by choosing a small
\epsilon>0
that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{W}(g_{0},f_{0})-\Theta_{m}(\Gamma)\right|\leq C(n,Y)e^{-\frac{3L^{2}}{16}}.

Since
L=\textbf{r}_{B,\sigma}^{|\Gamma|}
and
\mathcal{W}(g_{0},f_{0})=\mathcal{W}_{x_{0}^{*}}(1)
, this completes the proof.
∎

Next, we consider a closed Ricci flow
\mathcal{X}=\{M^{n},(g(t))_{t\in I}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
such that
[-10,0]\subset I
. Throughout, we fix a spacetime point
x_{0}^{*}=(x_{0},0)
, define
\tau=-t
, and set

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}=\mathrm{d}\nu_{x^{*}_{0};t}=(4\pi\tau)^{-\frac{n}{2}}e^{-f}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g(t)},\\
&\Phi=\frac{g}{2}-\tau\left(\mathrm{Ric}+\nabla^{2}f\right),\\
&F=\tau f.\end{cases}

Moreover, we define
b=2\sqrt{\left|f(-1)\right|}
.

Definition 8.4

(
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}^{|\Gamma|}
-radius)
.

For the weighted Riemannian manifold
\left(M,g(-1),f(-1)\right)
,
\textbf{r}_{C,\delta}^{|\Gamma|}
is defined as the largest number
L
for which there exists a smooth covering map
\varphi_{C}
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq L\}
onto a subset of
M
such that
f\left(\varphi_{C}(\bar{p}),-1\right)\leq n
and

\displaystyle\left[\bar{g}-\varphi_{C}^{*}g(-1)\right]_{2}\leq\delta.

(8.4)

Moreover, for
x\in U
with
\bar{f}(x)\geq 10n+\log|\Gamma|
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\delta)\left(\bar{f}(x)-\log|\Gamma|\right)\leq f(-1)\circ\varphi_{C}(x)\leq(1+\delta)\left(\bar{f}(x)-\log|\Gamma|\right).

(8.5)

By similar arguments as in Propositions
6.2
,
6.3
and Corollary
6.6
, we have the following result.

Proposition 8.5
.

For any small
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\bar{\delta}=\bar{\delta}(n,\epsilon,|\Gamma|)>0
such that if
\delta\leq\bar{\delta}
and
\textbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}\geq\bar{\delta}^{-2}
, then the following statements hold.

(i)

On
\left\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}-\bar{\delta}^{-1}\right\}\times[-9,-\epsilon]
,

\displaystyle\left[\varphi_{C}^{*}g(t)-\bar{g}(t)\right]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}\leq\epsilon,

where the norm
[\cdot]_{[\epsilon^{-1}]}
is taken with respect to
\bar{g}(t)
.

(ii)

For
(x,t)\in\left\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}-\bar{\delta}^{-1}\right\}\times[-1-\bar{\delta},-1+\bar{\delta}]
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\bar{F}(x)-C(n,Y,\epsilon)\leq F(\varphi_{C}(x),t)\leq(1+\epsilon)\bar{F}(x)+C(n,Y,\epsilon).

(8.6)

Moreover, for any
1\leq l\leq\epsilon^{-1}
,

\displaystyle[\varphi_{C}^{*}F]_{l}\leq C(n,Y,\epsilon)e^{\epsilon l\bar{F}}

on
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}-2\bar{\delta}^{-1}\}\times[-1-\bar{\delta},-1+\bar{\delta}]
.

Proof.

(i) follows from the same argument as in Proposition
6.2
, so we focus on (ii).
It suffices to prove (
8.6
); once established, the higher-order estimates follow exactly as in Corollary
6.6
.

By the same reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
6.3
(see Claim
6.5
), we have

\displaystyle d_{t}(p,\varphi_{C}(x))\leq(1+\epsilon)\bar{d}_{t}(\bar{p},x),

for any
(x,t)\in\left\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}-\bar{\delta}^{-1}\right\}\times[-9,-\epsilon]
, where
p=\varphi_{C}(\bar{p})
. This immediately yields the upper bound in (
8.6
) by the same argument as in Proposition
6.3
.

However, the lower bound in (
8.6
) cannot be obtained directly. Instead, we establish the following: for any
(x,t)\in\left\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}-\bar{\delta}^{-1}\right\}\times[-1-\bar{\delta},-1+\bar{\delta}]
,

\displaystyle d_{t}(p,\varphi_{C}(x))\geq(1-\epsilon)\bar{d}_{t}(\bar{p},x)-C(n,Y,\epsilon).

(8.7)

We first treat the case
t=-1
. It suffices to consider
x\in\left\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}-\bar{\delta}^{-1}\right\}
with
\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x)\gg 1
. For such
x
, (
8.5
) implies that any
x^{\prime}\in\varphi_{C}^{-1}(\varphi_{C}(x))
satisfies

\displaystyle(1-2\delta)\bar{f}(x^{\prime})\leq\bar{f}(x)\leq(1+2\delta)\bar{f}(x^{\prime}),

which implies

\displaystyle(1-3\delta)\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x)\leq\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x^{\prime})\leq(1+3\delta)\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x),

(8.8)

provided
\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x)\geq C(n,Y,\delta)
.

Let
\gamma(s)
, for
s\in[0,L]
, be a minimizing geodesic with respect to
g(-1)
from
p
to
\varphi_{C}(x)
. Set
s_{0}\in[0,L]
to be the smallest parameter so that
\gamma(s_{0})
lies on the boundary of
\varphi_{C}\left(\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}\}\right)
; if no such
s_{0}
exists, set
s_{0}=L
. Let
\tilde{\gamma}
denote the lift of
\gamma|_{[0,s_{0}]}
starting from
\bar{p}
under
\varphi_{C}
.

If
s_{0}<L
, it is clear from our definition of
s_{0}
that the length of
\tilde{\gamma}
with respect to
\bar{g}(-1)
is at least
\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}
. Thus, we obtain by (
8.4
)

\displaystyle d_{-1}(p,\varphi_{C}(x))\geq(1-\delta)\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}\geq(1-\delta)\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x).

If
s_{0}=L
, then
\tilde{\gamma}
is a curve from
\bar{p}
to some
x^{\prime}\in\varphi_{C}^{-1}(\varphi_{C}(x))
. Thus, it follows from (
8.4
) and (
8.8
) that

\displaystyle d_{-1}(p,\varphi_{C}(x))\geq(1-\delta)\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x^{\prime})\geq(1-3\delta)^{2}\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},x).

Therefore, (
8.7
) holds for
t=-1
if
\delta\leq\delta(\epsilon)
. The general case for
t\in[-1-\bar{\delta},-1+\bar{\delta}]
follows from the standard distance distortion estimate on
\varphi_{C}(U)\times[-1-\bar{\delta},-1+\bar{\delta}]
. Here, it suffices to consider minimizing geodesics from
p
to
x
lying entirely within
\varphi_{C}\left(\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\delta}\}\right)
; otherwise, (
8.7
) follows by the same argument as above.

Having (
8.7
), we can obtain the lower bound in (
8.6
) as in the proof of Proposition
6.3
.
∎

Next, we define

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}_{l}:=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\delta}(n,10^{-100}n^{-1}l^{-1}\sigma,|\Gamma|),

where
\bar{\delta}
is from Proposition
8.5
. By this choice, we can generalize Theorem
6.14
for the current case.

Theorem 8.6
.

For any constants
D>1
and
\sigma\in(0,1/10)
, then there exists a large constant
L^{\prime}=L^{\prime}(n,Y,\sigma,|\Gamma|,l,D)>1
satisfying the following property.

Let
\varphi_{A}
be the map corresponding to
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}
in Definition
5.1
. If
\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{A}\in[L^{\prime},(1-\sigma)\mathbf{r}_{E}]
, where
\textbf{r}_{E}
is defined as in Definition
6.9
, then there exists a covering map
\varphi
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{A}+D\}
onto a subset of
M
such that
\varphi=\varphi_{A}
on
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}-2\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}\}
and

\displaystyle\left[\varphi^{*}g(-1)-\bar{g}\right]_{2}\leq\bar{\delta}_{l}

on
U
. Moreover, if
x\in U
satisfies
\bar{f}(x)\geq 10n+\log|\Gamma|
, then

\displaystyle(1-\bar{\delta}_{l})\left(\bar{f}(x)-\log|\Gamma|\right)\leq f(-1)\circ\varphi_{C}(x)\leq(1+\bar{\delta}_{l})\left(\bar{f}(x)-\log|\Gamma|\right).

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\bar{\delta}_{l}}\geq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{A}+D.

Proof.

By the definitions of
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|}
and
\textbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\bar{\delta}_{l}}
, we conclude that
\textbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{C,\bar{\delta}_{l}}\geq\mathbf{r}^{|\Gamma|}_{A}-1
, provided that
L^{\prime}
is sufficiently large.

Suppose the conclusion fails. Then there exists a sequence of Ricci flows
\mathcal{X}^{i}=\{M^{n}_{i},(g_{i}(t))_{t\in[-10,0]}\}
with entropy bounded below by
-Y
. Moreover, there exist base points
x_{0,i}^{*}=(x_{0,i},0)\in\mathcal{X}^{i}
such that
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|,i}\to+\infty
and
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|,i}\leq(1-\sigma)\mathbf{r}^{i}_{E}
. For each
i
, there exists a covering map
\varphi_{A,i}
of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain
U_{i}
containing
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|,i}\}
onto a subset of
M
corresponding to
\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|,i}
in Definition
8.1
. However, it is not possible to find a covering map from a domain containing
\{\bar{b}\leq\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|,i}+D_{0}\}
onto a subset of
M_{i}
for some constant
D_{0}>0
that satisfies the required properties.

We define
D_{1}:=D_{0}+2\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}
,
L_{i}:=\textbf{r}_{A}^{|\Gamma|,i}-2\bar{\delta}_{l}^{-1}
and the hypersurface
\Sigma_{i}:=\varphi_{A,i}(U_{i})\cap\left(\{b_{i}=L_{i}-1\}\right)
, where
b_{i}:=2\sqrt{|f_{i}(-1)|}
. Moreover, we set
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}:=\varphi_{A,i}^{-1}(\Sigma_{i})
and denote by
\Omega_{i}(-1)
the domain enclosed by
\Sigma_{i}
. Let
\Omega_{i}=\Omega_{i}(-1)\times[-2,-1/2]
. It is clear that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}\subset\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}\}\setminus\{\bar{b}\geq L_{i}-2\}.

We choose a base point
q_{i}^{*}=(q_{i},-1/2)\in\mathcal{X}^{i}
such that
q_{i}\in\Sigma_{i}
.

Define the time intervals
\mathbb{I}^{++}=[-10,0]
,
\mathbb{I}^{+}=[-9.9,0]
,
\mathbb{I}=[-9.8,0]
and
\mathbb{I}^{-}=(-9.8,0]
. Then, by Theorem
2.17
, passing to a subsequence if necessary, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{I}}^{i},d_{i}^{*},q_{i}^{*},\mathfrak{t}_{i}\right)\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathrm{pGH}\hskip 8.19447pt}\left(Z,d_{Z},q,\mathfrak{t}\right),

where
d_{i}^{*}
denotes the spacetime distance induced by
g_{i}(t)
(see Definition
2.16
). We assume that the regular part
\mathcal{R}
of
Z
carries a structure of Ricci flow spacetime
(\mathcal{R},\mathfrak{t},\partial_{\mathfrak{t}},g^{Z})
. Let
\phi_{i}
denote the diffeomorphisms given in Theorem
2.19
.

As in the proof of Theorem
6.14
,
(\Omega_{i},g_{i}(t),q_{i}^{*})
, via the diffeomorphisms
\phi_{i}
, converge smoothly to a domain
\Omega\subset Z
containing the point
q
. Moreover, for any
q_{i}^{\prime}\in\varphi_{A,i}^{-1}(q_{i})
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\varphi_{A,i}^{-1}\left(\Omega_{i}\right),\varphi_{A,i}^{*}(\phi_{i}^{-1})^{*}g^{Z}_{t},q_{i}^{\prime}\right)\xrightarrow[i\to\infty]{C^{\infty}}\left(\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m}\times[-2,-1/2],g_{c}(t),q_{\infty}^{\prime}\right),

where
g_{c}(t)
is a family of standard metrics on
\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m}
. By the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
6.14
, we conclude that
Z_{(-1-\epsilon,-1+\epsilon)}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{(-1-\epsilon,-1+\epsilon)}
, where
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}:=\iota_{q}(\mathcal{R}^{q})
and
\epsilon
is a small constant, depending on
n
,
Y
,
|\Gamma|
,
\sigma
and
l
.

In addition, there exists a vector field
V_{i}
such that on
B_{g_{i}(-1)}(\Sigma_{i},100D_{1})
such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert|V_{i}|-1\right\rvert\leq\Psi(i^{-1})\quad\text{and}\quad[\nabla_{i}V_{i}]_{100}\leq\Psi(i^{-1}).

Moreover, on
B_{g_{i}(-1)}(\Sigma_{i},100D_{1})
, the vector
\left(\varphi_{A,i}\right)^{-1}_{*}V_{i}
converge smoothly to the direction in the first
\mathbb{R}
of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m-1}\times S^{m}
. By using
V_{i}
, one can define
\varphi^{\prime}_{i}
and
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}
in exactly the same way as (
6.45
) and (
6.46
). Thus, it follows from the construction that
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is a smooth covering map of degree
|\Gamma|
from a domain containing
\{\bar{b}\leq L_{i}+20D_{1}\}
onto a subset of
M_{i}
such that

\displaystyle\left[(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime})^{*}g_{i}(-1)-\bar{g}(-1)\right]_{2}\leq\Psi(i^{-1}).

(8.9)

Moreover, for any
y
in the domain of
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}
with
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y)\in B_{g_{i}(-1)}(\Sigma_{i},100D_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\Psi(i^{-1}))\frac{L_{i}^{2}}{4}\leq f_{i}(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y),-1)\leq(1+\Psi(i^{-1}))\frac{L_{i}^{2}}{4}.

(8.10)

Indeed, the upper bound in (
8.10
) follows from (
8.9
) together with the argument used in the proof of Proposition
8.5
(ii). For the lower bound, the proof of Proposition
6.3
shows that for any
y
in the domain of
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}
with
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y)\in B_{g_{i}(-1)}(\Sigma_{i},100D_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle f_{i}(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y),-1)\geq(1-\Psi(i^{-1}))\frac{d^{2}_{i,-1}(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{p}),\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y))}{4},

(8.11)

where
d_{i,-1}
denotes the distance function of
g_{i}(-1)
. Moreover, for any
w
with
\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(w)\in\Sigma_{i}
, our definition of
L_{i}
yields

\displaystyle f_{i}(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(w),-1)=\frac{(L_{i}-1)^{2}}{4}.

In addition, by the same reasoning as in the derivation of (
8.7
), we have

\displaystyle d_{i,-1}(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{p}),\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(w))\geq(1-\Psi(i^{-1}))\bar{d}_{-1}(\bar{p},w)\geq(1-\Psi(i^{-1}))L_{i}.

Since
V_{i}
is almost a unit vector field, combining this with (
8.11
) gives

\displaystyle f_{i}(\varphi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(y),-1)\geq(1-\Psi(i^{-1}))\frac{L_{i}^{2}}{4},
